Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

Joe has been hurt on the Internet. Now he is storming around the house, destroying everything in his path.

Joe's house has n floors, each floor is a segment of m cells. Each cell either contains nothing (it is an empty cell), or has a brick or a concrete wall (always something one of three). It is believed that each floor is surrounded by a concrete wall on the left and on the right.

Now Joe is on the n-th floor and in the first cell, counting from left to right. At each moment of time, Joe has the direction of his gaze, to the right or to the left (always one direction of the two). Initially, Joe looks to the right.

Joe moves by a particular algorithm. Every second he makes one of the following actions:   If the cell directly under Joe is empty, then Joe falls down. That is, he moves to this cell, the gaze direction is preserved.  Otherwise consider the next cell in the current direction of the gaze.   If the cell is empty, then Joe moves into it, the gaze direction is preserved.  If this cell has bricks, then Joe breaks them with his forehead (the cell becomes empty), and changes the direction of his gaze to the opposite.  If this cell has a concrete wall, then Joe just changes the direction of his gaze to the opposite (concrete can withstand any number of forehead hits).  

Joe calms down as soon as he reaches any cell of the first floor.

The figure below shows an example Joe's movements around the house.

 [Image] 

Determine how many seconds Joe will need to calm down.


-----Input-----

The first line contains two integers n and m (2 ≤ n ≤ 100, 1 ≤ m ≤ 10^4).

Next n lines contain the description of Joe's house. The i-th of these lines contains the description of the (n - i + 1)-th floor of the house — a line that consists of m characters: "." means an empty cell, "+" means bricks and "#" means a concrete wall.

It is guaranteed that the first cell of the n-th floor is empty.


-----Output-----

Print a single number — the number of seconds Joe needs to reach the first floor; or else, print word "Never" (without the quotes), if it can never happen.

Please, do not use the %lld specifier to read or write 64-bit integers in С++. It is preferred to use the cin, cout streams or the %I64d specifier.


-----Examples-----
Input
3 5
..+.#
#+..+
+.#+.

Output
14
Input
4 10
...+.##+.+
+#++..+++#
++.#++++..
.+##.++#.+

Output
42

Input
2 2
..
++

Output
Never

The input will be stdin and you should print your solution to stdout

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. Joe starts on the n-th floor (bottom floor) in the first cell, looking right
2. Each second, Joe performs one of these actions:
   - If cell directly below is empty, he falls down
   - Otherwise, he looks in current direction and:
     - If empty cell: moves there (direction stays)
     - If brick (+): breaks it (becomes empty), changes direction
     - If concrete wall (#): changes direction only
3. Joe stops when he reaches any cell on the first floor
4. We need to find how many seconds it takes, or "Never" if impossible

## Key Insights

1. Joe can only reach the first floor by falling down from above
2. When he falls down, he moves to the cell directly below and keeps his direction
3. The movement pattern depends on whether he encounters empty cells, bricks, or walls
4. The state is defined by: current floor, current column, current direction
5. We need to detect cycles to avoid infinite loops

## Solution Strategy

1. Use BFS or simulation with state tracking
2. State: (floor, column, direction)
3. Simulate each second until Joe reaches floor 0 or we detect a cycle
4. Track visited states to detect cycles (infinite loops)

## Step-by-Step Implementation

```python
from collections import deque

def solve():
    n, m = map(int, input().split())
    
    # Read the floors - floors are given from bottom to top
    # floor i (0-indexed from bottom) has description at index i
    floors = []
    for _ in range(n):
        floors.append(input().strip())
    
    # Joe starts on bottom floor (n-1 in 0-indexed), column 0, direction = 1 (right)
    # direction: 1 = right, -1 = left
    
    # State: (floor, column, direction)
    # We'll use BFS with visited states to detect cycles
    queue = deque()
    visited = set()
    
    # Initial state
    start_state = (n - 1, 0, 1)  # (floor, col, direction)
    queue.append((n - 1, 0, 1, 0))  # (floor, col, dir, time)
    visited.add((n - 1, 0, 1))
    
    while queue:
        floor, col, direction, time = queue.popleft()
        
        # Check if we reached the first floor
        if floor == 0:
            return time
        
        # Try to fall down
        next_floor = floor - 1
        if next_floor >= 0 and floors[next_floor][col] == '.':
            # Can fall down
            new_state = (next_floor, col, direction)
            if new_state not in visited:
                visited.add(new_state)
                queue.append((next_floor, col, direction, time + 1))
        
        # Otherwise, look in current direction
        # Get next cell in current direction
        next_col = col
        if direction == 1:  # right
            next_col = col + 1
        else:  # left
            next_col = col - 1
        
        # Check bounds
        if next_col < 0 or next_col >= m:
            # If out of bounds, we hit concrete wall
            new_direction = -direction
            new_state = (floor, col, new_direction)
            if new_state not in visited:
                visited.add(new_state)
                queue.append((floor, col, new_direction, time + 1))
        else:
            cell = floors[floor][next_col]
            if cell == '.':
                # Empty cell - move there
                new_state = (floor, next_col, direction)
                if new_state not in visited:
                    visited.add(new_state)
                    queue.append((floor, next_col, direction, time + 1))
            elif cell == '+':
                # Brick - break it, change direction
                new_state = (floor, next_col, -direction)
                # Make the cell empty (change it to '.')
                # We need to update our representation - we can't modify original array directly
                # Instead, we'll track state and handle the breaking separately
                if new_state not in visited:
                    visited.add(new_state)
                    queue.append((floor, next_col, -direction, time + 1))
            else:  # cell == '#'
                # Concrete wall - change direction only
                new_direction = -direction
                new_state = (floor, col, new_direction)
                if new_state not in visited:
                    visited.add(new_state)
                    queue.append((floor, col, new_direction, time + 1))
    
    # If we exit the queue without reaching first floor, it's never
    return "Never"

print(solve())
```

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. Joe starts on the n-th floor (top floor) at position 0 (first cell), looking right
2. Each second, Joe performs one of three actions:
   - If cell directly below is empty, Joe falls down (moves to that cell, direction unchanged)
   - Otherwise, he looks in current direction:
     - If next cell is empty: moves there, direction unchanged
     - If next cell has bricks: breaks bricks (cell becomes empty), direction flips
     - If next cell has concrete wall: direction flips (wall is indestructible)
3. Joe stops when he reaches any cell on the first floor
4. We need to count seconds until he reaches first floor, or output "Never" if impossible

## Key Insights

1. This is a state-space search problem where each state is defined by:
   - Current floor (0 to n-1, where 0 is first floor)
   - Current position on that floor
   - Current direction (left or right)

2. We need to avoid infinite loops - if we visit a state we've seen before, we're in a cycle.

3. The state space is limited since:
   - Floor ranges from 0 to n-1
   - Position ranges from 0 to m-1
   - Direction is 2 states

## Algorithm

1. Use BFS or DFS with memoization to explore states
2. For each state, simulate Joe's movement according to the rules
3. Return the number of seconds when we reach floor 0
4. If we detect a cycle (already visited state), return "Never"

## Implementation Steps

1. Parse input
2. Use a queue for BFS with state (floor, position, direction, time)
3. Track visited states to detect cycles
4. For each state, apply the movement rules
5. Return time when floor reaches 0

```python
from collections import deque

def solve():
    n, m = map(int, input().split())
    
    # Read floors - floors are given from n-th to 1st
    floors = []
    for _ in range(n):
        floors.append(input().strip())
    
    # Convert floors to 0-indexed: floor 0 is first floor, floor n-1 is n-th floor
    # So floors[i] represents the (n-i)-th floor
    
    # State: (floor, position, direction, time)
    # direction: 0 = left, 1 = right
    # We need to detect cycles to avoid infinite loops
    
    visited = set()
    queue = deque()
    
    # Start at n-th floor (index n-1), position 0, direction right (1)
    start_state = (n-1, 0, 1, 0)
    queue.append(start_state)
    visited.add((n-1, 0, 1))
    
    while queue:
        floor, pos, direction, time = queue.popleft()
        
        # Check if we reached first floor
        if floor == 0:
            return time
        
        # Get current cell content
        cell_content = floors[floor][pos]
        
        # Try to fall down
        if floor < n - 1:  # Can fall to next floor down
            next_floor = floor + 1
            if next_floor >= n:
                continue
                
            # Check if cell directly below is empty
            below_cell = floors[next_floor][pos]
            if below_cell == '.':
                # Fall down
                new_state = (next_floor, pos, direction, time + 1)
                if (next_floor, pos, direction) not in visited:
                    visited.add((next_floor, pos, direction))
                    queue.append(new_state)
            else:
                # Can't fall, need to move in current direction
                next_pos = pos
                if direction == 1:  # right
                    next_pos = pos + 1
                else:  # left
                    next_pos = pos - 1
                
                # Check if next cell is valid and can be accessed
                if next_pos < 0 or next_pos >= m:
                    # Out of bounds - should not happen with concrete walls
                    # But if we have concrete wall, direction flips
                    if direction == 1:  # right
                        new_dir = 0
                    else:  # left
                        new_dir = 1
                    new_state = (floor, pos, new_dir, time + 1)
                    if (floor, pos, new_dir) not in visited:
                        visited.add((floor, pos, new_dir))
                        queue.append(new_state)
                else:
                    next_cell_content = floors[floor][next_pos]
                    if next_cell_content == '.':
                        # Move to empty cell
                        new_state = (floor, next_pos, direction, time + 1)
                        if (floor, next_pos, direction) not in visited:
                            visited.add((floor, next_pos, direction))
                            queue.append(new_state)
                    elif next_cell_content == '+':
                        # Break brick, direction flips
                        new_dir = 1 - direction
                        new_state = (floor, next_pos, new_dir, time + 1)
                        if (floor, next_pos, new_dir) not in visited:
                            visited.add((floor, next_pos, new_dir))
                            queue.append(new_state)
                    else:  # next_cell_content == '#'
                        # Concrete wall - direction flips
                        new_dir = 1 - direction
                        new_state = (floor, pos, new_dir, time + 1)
                        if (floor, pos, new_dir) not in visited:
                            visited.add((floor, pos, new_dir))
                            queue.append(new_state)
    
    return "Never"

print(solve())
```

```python
from collections import deque

def solve():
    n, m = map(int, input().split())
    
    # Read floors - floors are given from n-th to 1st
    floors = []
    for _ in range(n):
        floors.append(input().strip())
    
    # State: (floor, position, direction, time)
    # direction: 0 = left, 1 = right
    visited = set()
    queue = deque()
    
    # Start at n-th floor (index n-1), position 0, direction right (1)
    start_state = (n-1, 0, 1, 0)
    queue.append(start_state)
    visited.add((n-1, 0, 1))
    
    while queue:
        floor, pos, direction, time = queue.popleft()
        
        # Check if we reached first floor
        if floor == 0:
            return time
        
        # Check if we can fall down
        if floor < n - 1:  # Can fall to floor+1
            below_floor = floor + 1
            below_pos = pos
            below_cell = floors[below_floor][below_pos]
            
            if below_cell == '.':
                # Fall down to the cell directly below
                new_state = (below_floor, below_pos, direction, time + 1)
                if (below_floor, below_pos, direction) not in visited:
                    visited.add((below_floor, below_pos, direction))
                    queue.append(new_state)
            else:
                # Can't fall down, need to move in current direction
                next_pos = pos
                if direction == 1:  # right
                    next_pos = pos + 1
                else:  # left
                    next_pos = pos - 1
                
                # Check bounds
                if next_pos < 0 or next_pos >= m:
                    # Out of bounds - treat as concrete wall
                    new_dir = 1 - direction
                    new_state = (floor, pos, new_dir, time + 1)
                    if (floor, pos, new_dir) not in visited:
                        visited.add((floor, pos, new_dir))
                        queue.append(new_state)
                else:
                    next_cell_content = floors[floor][next_pos]
                    if next_cell_content == '.':
                        # Move to empty cell

    return "Never"
```

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

Joe has been hurt on the Internet. Now he is storming around the house, destroying everything in his path.

Joe's house has n floors, each floor is a segment of m cells. Each cell either contains nothing (it is an empty cell), or has a brick or a concrete wall (always something one of three). It is believed that each floor is surrounded by a concrete wall on the left and on the right.

Now Joe is on the n-th floor and in the first cell, counting from left to right. At each moment of time, Joe has the direction of his gaze, to the right or to the left (always one direction of the two). Initially, Joe looks to the right.

Joe moves by a particular algorithm. Every second he makes one of the following actions:   If the cell directly under Joe is empty, then Joe falls down. That is, he moves to this cell, the gaze direction is preserved.  Otherwise consider the next cell in the current direction of the gaze.   If the cell is empty, then Joe moves into it, the gaze direction is preserved.  If this cell has bricks, then Joe breaks them with his forehead (the cell becomes empty), and changes the direction of his gaze to the opposite.  If this cell has a concrete wall, then Joe just changes the direction of his gaze to the opposite (concrete can withstand any number of forehead hits).  

Joe calms down as soon as he reaches any cell of the first floor.

The figure below shows an example Joe's movements around the house.

 [Image] 

Determine how many seconds Joe will need to calm down.


-----Input-----

The first line contains two integers n and m (2 ≤ n ≤ 100, 1 ≤ m ≤ 10^4).

Next n lines contain the description of Joe's house. The i-th of these lines contains the description of the (n - i + 1)-th floor of the house — a line that consists of m characters: "." means an empty cell, "+" means bricks and "#" means a concrete wall.

It is guaranteed that the first cell of the n-th floor is empty.


-----Output-----

Print a single number — the number of seconds Joe needs to reach the first floor; or else, print word "Never" (without the quotes), if it can never happen.

Please, do not use the %lld specifier to read or write 64-bit integers in С++. It is preferred to use the cin, cout streams or the %I64d specifier.


-----Examples-----
Input
3 5
..+.#
#+..+
+.#+.

Output
14
Input
4 10
...+.##+.+
+#++..+++#
++.#++++..
.+##.++#.+

Output
42

Input
2 2
..
++

Output
Never

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. Joe starts on the n-th floor (bottom floor) at the first cell (leftmost) and looks to the right initially.
2. Each second, Joe performs one of these actions:
   - If the cell directly below him is empty, he falls down to that cell (direction stays the same)
   - Otherwise, he looks in his current direction:
     - If the next cell in that direction is empty, he moves there (direction stays)
     - If the next cell has bricks, he breaks them (cell becomes empty), changes direction
     - If the next cell has a concrete wall, he changes direction only
3. Joe stops when he reaches any cell on the first floor.
4. We need to count the seconds until this happens, or return "Never" if it never happens.

## Key Insights

1. This is a simulation problem where we need to track Joe's position, direction, and the state of the grid.
2. We need to detect cycles to avoid infinite loops (return "Never").
3. The state can be represented by (floor, column, direction).
4. If we encounter the same state twice, we've entered a cycle and it will never reach the first floor.

## Step-by-Step Solution

1. Parse input to get the grid
2. Initialize Joe's position (starting at bottom-left, looking right)
3. Use a set to track visited states (floor, col, direction) to detect cycles
4. Simulate each second:
   - Check if current position is on first floor → return seconds
   - Check if cell below is empty → fall down
   - Otherwise, look in current direction:
     - If next cell is empty → move there
     - If next cell has bricks → break bricks, change direction
     - If next cell has concrete wall → change direction
5. If we detect a cycle, return "Never"

```python
def solve():
    n, m = map(int, input().split())
    
    # Read the grid
    grid = []
    for _ in range(n):
        line = input().strip()
        grid.append(list(line))
    
    # Joe starts at floor n-1 (0-indexed), column 0, looking right (1 for right, 0 for left)
    floor = n - 1
    col = 0
    direction = 1  # 1 = right, 0 = left
    
    # Set to track visited states to detect cycles
    visited = set()
    seconds = 0
    
    while floor > 0:  # While not on first floor (0-indexed)
        # Create state tuple for cycle detection
        state = (floor, col, direction)
        if state in visited:
            print("Never")
            return
        
        visited.add(state)
        
        # Check if we can fall down
        below_floor = floor - 1
        if below_floor >= 0 and grid[below_floor][col] == '.':
            # Fall down to the cell below
            floor -= 1
            seconds += 1
        else:
            # Look in current direction
            next_col = col
            if direction == 1:  # moving right
                next_col = col + 1
            else:  # moving left
                next_col = col - 1
            
            # Check bounds and grid content
            if next_col < 0 or next_col >= m:
                # This shouldn't happen based on problem constraints, but just in case
                if direction == 1:
                    direction = 0  # change to left
                else:
                    direction = 1  # change to right
            else:
                cell = grid[floor][next_col]
                if cell == '.':
                    # Move to empty cell
                    col = next_col
                    seconds += 1
                elif cell == '+':
                    # Break brick, change direction
                    grid[floor][next_col] = '.'  # break brick
                    direction = 1 - direction  # change direction
                    seconds += 1
                elif cell == '#':
                    # Concrete wall, just change direction
                    direction = 1 - direction
                    seconds += 1
        
        # Check if we've reached first floor
        if floor == 0:
            break
    
    print(seconds)

solve()
```

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. Joe starts on the n-th floor (bottom floor) at position 0 (first cell) looking right
2. Each second, Joe performs one of these actions:
   - If the cell directly below him is empty: he falls down to that cell (direction remains same)
   - Otherwise: he looks in his current direction and:
     - If next cell is empty: he moves there (direction remains same)
     - If next cell has bricks: he breaks bricks (cell becomes empty), flips direction
     - If next cell has concrete wall: he flips direction (wall is unbreakable)
3. Joe stops when he reaches any cell on the first floor
4. If Joe never reaches the first floor, output "Never"

## Key Observations

1. Joe will eventually reach the first floor unless he gets trapped in an infinite loop
2. We need to track Joe's position, direction, and the state of the grid
3. Since the grid is finite, if Joe revisits a state (position, direction), we have a loop
4. We should detect cycles to avoid infinite processing

## Solution Approach

1. Use BFS or simulation with state tracking
2. State = (floor, position, direction)
3. For each state, simulate one second of movement
4. If we reach floor 0, return the time
5. If we detect a repeated state, return "Never"
6. Otherwise, continue until we reach floor 0

## Step-by-step Implementation

```python
from collections import defaultdict

def solve():
    n, m = map(int, input().split())
    
    # Read the floors (n-th floor is bottom)
    floors = []
    for _ in range(n):
        floors.append(input().strip())
    
    # Convert to 0-indexed: floor 0 is top, floor n-1 is bottom
    # But Joe starts at bottom (n-1), so we need to access floors[n-1] for bottom
    
    # State: (floor, position, direction)
    # direction: 0 = left, 1 = right
    # We'll use a visited set to detect cycles
    
    visited = set()
    time = 0
    floor = n - 1  # start at bottom floor (0-indexed from top)
    pos = 0        # start at first cell (position 0)
    direction = 1  # start looking right (1 = right, 0 = left)
    
    while floor > 0:  # while not on first floor (floor 0 in 0-indexed)
        state = (floor, pos, direction)
        if state in visited:
            print("Never")
            return
            
        visited.add(state)
        
        # Check if the cell below is empty - if so, fall down
        if floor > 0 and floors[floor - 1][pos] == '.':
            floor -= 1
            time += 1
            continue
        
        # Otherwise, look in current direction
        next_pos = pos
        next_direction = direction
        
        if direction == 1:  # looking right
            next_pos = pos + 1
        else:  # looking left
            next_pos = pos - 1
        
        # Check if next position is valid
        if next_pos < 0 or next_pos >= m:
            # This shouldn't happen with walls, but let's handle it
            # Actually, if out of bounds, it should be a wall
            # For walls, we just flip direction
            direction = 1 - direction
            time += 1
            continue
        
        cell = floors[floor][next_pos]
        
        if cell == '.':
            # Move to empty cell
            pos = next_pos
            time += 1
        elif cell == '+':
            # Bricks - break them and flip direction
            # Update the grid - we can't actually modify the original string
            # So we need to simulate without modifying the state
            # But we don't need to store grid state, just the current configuration
            # Actually, we don't need to modify the actual grid since we're simulating
            direction = 1 - direction
            time += 1
        elif cell == '#':
            # Concrete wall - just flip direction
            direction = 1 - direction
            time += 1
    
    # If we reach here, we're on floor 0 (first floor)
    print(time)

solve()
```

```python
n, m = map(int, input().split())

# Read floors: floors[i] represents floor (i+1) from bottom
# floors[0] = bottom floor (n-th floor)
# floors[1] = (n-1)th floor
# ...
# floors[n-1] = top floor (1st floor)

floors = []
for _ in range(n):
    floors.append(input().strip())

# Joe starts at bottom floor (index 0), position 0, direction right (1)
visited = set()
time = 0
floor = 0  # bottom floor
pos = 0
direction = 1  # 1 = right, 0 = left

while floor < n - 1:  # while not on top floor (last floor)
    state = (floor, pos, direction)
    if state in visited:
        print("Never")
        exit()
        
    visited.add(state)
    
    # Check if can fall down
    if floor < n - 1 and floors[floor + 1][pos] == '.':
        floor += 1
        time += 1
    else:
        # Move in current direction
        next_pos = pos
        if direction == 1:  # right
            next_pos = pos + 1
        else:  # left
            next_pos = pos - 1
        
        # Check bounds - if out of bounds, it's a wall
        if next_pos < 0 or next_pos >= m:
            direction = 1 - direction
            time += 1
        else:
            cell = floors[floor][next_pos]
            if cell == '.':
                pos = next_pos
                time += 1
            elif cell == '+':
                direction = 1 - direction
                time += 1
            elif cell == '#':
```

pos = 0
direction = 1  # 1 = right, 0 = left

    if state in visited:
        print("Never")
        exit()
        
    visited.add(state)

    if floor < n - 1 and floors[floor + 1][pos] == '.':

        else:  # left

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

Joe has been hurt on the Internet. Now he is storming around the house, destroying everything in his path.

Joe's house has n floors, each floor is a segment of m cells. Each cell either contains nothing (it is an empty cell), or has a brick or a concrete wall (always something one of three). It is believed that each floor is surrounded by a concrete wall on the left and on the right.

Now Joe is on the n-th floor and in the first cell, counting from left to right. At each moment of time, Joe has the direction of his gaze, to the right or to the left (always one direction of the two). Initially, Joe looks to the right.

Joe moves by a particular algorithm. Every second he makes one of the following actions:   If the cell directly under Joe is empty, then Joe falls down. That is, he moves to this cell, the gaze direction is preserved.  Otherwise consider the next cell in the current direction of the gaze.   If the cell is empty, then Joe moves into it, the gaze direction is preserved.  If this cell has bricks, then Joe breaks them with his forehead (the cell becomes empty), and changes the direction of his gaze to the opposite.  If this cell has a concrete wall, then Joe just changes the direction of his gaze to the opposite (concrete can withstand any number of forehead hits).  

Joe calms down as soon as he reaches any cell of the first floor.

The figure below shows an example Joe's movements around the house.

 [Image] 

Determine how many seconds Joe will need to calm down.


-----Input-----

The first line contains two integers n and m (2 ≤ n ≤ 100, 1 ≤ m ≤ 10^4).

Next n lines contain the description of Joe's house. The i-th of these lines contains the description of the (n - i + 1)-th floor of the house — a line that consists of m characters: "." means an empty cell, "+" means bricks and "#" means a concrete wall.

It is guaranteed that the first cell of the n-th floor is empty.


-----Output-----

Print a single number — the number of seconds Joe needs to reach the first floor; or else, print word "Never" (without the quotes), if it can never happen.

Please, do not use the %lld specifier to read or write 64-bit integers in С++. It is preferred to use the cin, cout streams or the %I64d specifier.


-----Examples-----
Input
3 5
..+.#
#+..+
+.#+.

Output
14
Input
4 10
...+.##+.+
+#++..+++#
++.#++++..
.+##.++#.+

Output
42

Input
2 2
..
++

Output
Never

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. Joe starts at floor n (bottom) in the first cell, looking right
2. Each second, Joe performs one action:
   - If the cell directly below him is empty, he falls down
   - Otherwise, he moves in his current direction:
     - If empty cell: moves there (direction preserved)
     - If brick (+): breaks it (becomes empty), direction flips
     - If concrete wall (#): direction flips
3. Joe stops when he reaches any cell on the first floor
4. We need to find the total seconds or output "Never" if impossible

## Key Insights

1. Joe can only stop when he reaches floor 1
2. The movement can potentially loop indefinitely if Joe keeps flipping directions without making progress
3. We need to simulate the process with state tracking
4. We need to detect cycles to avoid infinite loops

## State Representation

The state consists of:
- Current floor (0 to n-1, where 0 is first floor)
- Current position (0 to m-1)
- Current direction (0 = left, 1 = right)

## Simulation Algorithm

1. Start at floor n-1 (0-indexed), position 0, direction right
2. For each second:
   - Check if current floor is 0 (first floor): return current time
   - If cell directly below is empty: fall down (go to next floor, same position)
   - Otherwise: try to move in current direction
     - If target cell is empty: move there
     - If target cell is brick: break it, flip direction
     - If target cell is concrete wall: flip direction
3. If we enter a state we've seen before, it's a cycle → "Never"
4. If we reach floor 0, return the time

## Implementation

```python
def solve():
    n, m = map(int, input().split())
    
    # Read the floors (n-th floor is last in input)
    floors = []
    for _ in range(n):
        floors.append(input().strip())
    
    # Convert to 0-indexed: floor 0 is the first floor (top), floor n-1 is the last floor (bottom)
    # So Joe starts at floor n-1, position 0, direction right
    
    # State: (floor, position, direction)
    # direction: 0 = left, 1 = right
    visited = set()
    
    floor = n - 1  # start at bottom floor (0-indexed)
    pos = 0        # start at first position
    direction = 1  # start looking right (1 = right, 0 = left)
    time = 0
    
    while floor > 0:  # while not on first floor
        state = (floor, pos, direction)
        
        # Check for cycle
        if state in visited:
            print("Never")
            return
            
        visited.add(state)
        
        # Check if we can fall down
        below_floor = floor - 1
        below_pos = pos
        
        if floors[below_floor][below_pos] == '.':
            # Fall down to next floor
            floor -= 1
            time += 1
        else:
            # Move in current direction
            next_pos = pos
            if direction == 1:  # right
                next_pos = pos + 1
            else:  # left
                next_pos = pos - 1
            
            # Check if next position is valid
            if next_pos < 0 or next_pos >= m:
                # This should not happen with the wall constraints
                # But if it does, we flip direction
                direction = 1 - direction
                time += 1
            else:
                cell = floors[below_floor][below_pos]  # Wait, no!
                # I made a mistake. The current cell is at (floor, pos)
                # We need to check the cell in the current direction, not below
                
                # Correction: we need to look at the cell in the current direction at current floor
                # and check what happens there
                
                current_cell = floors[floor][next_pos]
                
                if current_cell == '.':
                    # Move to that cell
                    pos = next_pos
                    time += 1
                elif current_cell == '+':
                    # Break brick, flip direction
                    # Change cell to empty
                    floors[floor] = floors[floor][:next_pos] + '.' + floors[floor][next_pos+1:]
                    direction = 1 - direction
                    time += 1
                elif current_cell == '#':
                    # Concrete wall, flip direction
                    direction = 1 - direction
                    time += 1
    
    # If we reach here, we're on first floor
    print(time)

solve()
```

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. Joe starts on the nth floor (bottom) in the first cell, looking right initially
2. Each second, Joe performs one of these actions:
   - If cell directly below is empty, he falls down (moves to that cell, direction stays)
   - Else, he looks in current direction and:
     - If next cell is empty, he moves there (direction stays)
     - If next cell has bricks, he breaks them (cell becomes empty), changes direction
     - If next cell has concrete wall, he changes direction only
3. Joe stops when he reaches any cell on the first floor
4. We need to count the number of seconds until he stops

## Key Observations

1. Joe moves in a specific algorithm - he either falls down or moves horizontally
2. When he hits bricks, he breaks them and changes direction
3. When he hits concrete walls, he only changes direction
4. The simulation might loop infinitely (return "Never")

## Algorithm Approach

1. Use simulation with state tracking
2. State: (floor, column, direction)
3. For each second, apply the movement rules
4. Check for cycles to detect "Never"
5. Stop when Joe reaches floor 1

## Step-by-step Solution

1. Parse input to get the house configuration
2. Initialize state: floor = n, col = 0, direction = right (1), time = 0
3. Use a visited set to detect cycles
4. In each iteration:
   - Check if already visited this state (cycle detected → "Never")
   - Apply movement rules
   - If reached floor 1, return time
   - Otherwise continue

Let me trace through the first example:
- n=3, m=5
- Floor 3 (bottom): "..+.#" → index 0,1,2,3,4
- Floor 2: "#+..+" 
- Floor 1 (top): "+.#+."

Joe starts at floor 3, col 0, right direction.

```python
def solve():
    n, m = map(int, input().split())
    
    # Read floors - store from bottom to top (n-th floor is bottom)
    floors = []
    for _ in range(n):
        floors.append(input().strip())
    
    # Convert to list of lists for easier access
    # floors[i] represents the (n-i)-th floor from bottom (i=0 is bottom)
    # So floor 0 is bottom, floor n-1 is top
    
    # State: (floor, col, direction) where direction = 1 for right, -1 for left
    # We'll use a set to detect cycles
    visited = set()
    time = 0
    floor = 0  # starting at bottom (0-indexed from bottom)
    col = 0
    direction = 1  # 1 for right, -1 for left
    
    # Check if we've already reached first floor
    while floor > 0:
        # Create state key for cycle detection
        state = (floor, col, direction)
        if state in visited:
            print("Never")
            return
            
        visited.add(state)
        
        # Check if current cell below is empty - move down
        below_col = col
        if floor < n - 1:  # not at top floor
            if floors[floor + 1][below_col] == '.':
                floor += 1
                time += 1
                continue
        
        # No cell below to fall to, so move horizontally
        next_col = col + direction
        
        # Check if next cell in current direction exists
        if next_col < 0 or next_col >= m:
            # Reached boundary - change direction only
            direction *= -1
            time += 1
            continue
        
        cell = floors[floor][next_col]
        
        if cell == '.':
            # Empty cell - move there
            col = next_col
            time += 1
        elif cell == '+':
            # Brick - break it, change direction
            # Convert brick to empty
            floors[floor] = floors[floor][:next_col] + '.' + floors[floor][next_col + 1:]
            direction *= -1
            col = next_col
            time += 1
        elif cell == '#':
            # Concrete wall - change direction only
            direction *= -1
            time += 1
    
    print(time)

solve()
```

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. Joe starts on the n-th floor (top floor) at the first cell, looking right
2. Each second, Joe performs one of three actions:
   - If cell directly below is empty, he falls down (moves to that cell, direction unchanged)
   - Otherwise, he looks in his current direction and:
     - If next cell in that direction is empty, he moves there (direction unchanged)
     - If next cell has bricks, he breaks them (cell becomes empty), changes direction
     - If next cell has concrete wall, he changes direction only
3. Joe stops when he reaches any cell on the first floor
4. We need to find how many seconds it takes, or "Never" if it never happens

## Key Insights

1. This is a simulation problem with state space that can potentially be infinite
2. We need to detect cycles (when Joe goes through the same state with the same direction)
3. A state is defined by: (floor, position, direction)
4. If we encounter a repeated state, we have a cycle and it will never reach the first floor
5. We need to simulate Joe's movement second by second

## Step-by-Step Solution

1. Parse input and store the floor configuration
2. Initialize Joe's position (start at bottom floor, position 0), direction (right)
3. Use a set to track visited states to detect cycles
4. For each second:
   - Check if current position is on first floor → return seconds
   - If cell below is empty → fall down
   - Otherwise, look in current direction:
     - If empty cell → move there
     - If brick → break brick, change direction
     - If concrete wall → change direction only
5. If we detect a repeated state, return "Never"

## Implementation

```python
def solve():
    n, m = map(int, input().split())
    
    # Read the floors - floors are given from top to bottom
    # floor i (0-indexed) corresponds to (n-i)th floor from bottom
    floors = []
    for _ in range(n):
        floors.append(input().strip())
    
    # Joe starts at floor n-1 (bottom floor), position 0, looking right
    floor = n - 1
    pos = 0
    direction = 1  # 1 for right, -1 for left
    
    # Track visited states to detect cycles
    visited = set()
    
    seconds = 0
    
    while floor > 0:  # While not on first floor (0-indexed)
        # Create state tuple for visited set
        state = (floor, pos, direction)
        
        # Check for cycle
        if state in visited:
            print("Never")
            return
        
        visited.add(state)
        
        # Check if cell below is empty - if so, fall down
        if floor > 0 and floors[floor - 1][pos] == '.':
            floor -= 1
        else:
            # Look in current direction
            next_pos = pos
            next_direction = direction
            
            # Check next cell in current direction
            if direction == 1:  # looking right
                next_pos = pos + 1
                if next_pos >= m:
                    # Hit right wall - change direction
                    direction = -1
                else:
                    if floors[floor][next_pos] == '.':
                        # Empty cell - move there
                        pos = next_pos
                    elif floors[floor][next_pos] == '+':
                        # Brick - break it, change direction
                        floors[floor] = floors[floor][:next_pos] + '.' + floors[floor][next_pos + 1:]
                        direction = -direction
                    elif floors[floor][next_pos] == '#':
                        # Concrete wall - change direction only
                        direction = -direction
            else:  # looking left
                next_pos = pos - 1
                if next_pos < 0:
                    # Hit left wall - change direction
                    direction = 1
                else:
                    if floors[floor][next_pos] == '.':
                        # Empty cell - move there
                        pos = next_pos
                    elif floors[floor][next_pos] == '+':
                        # Brick - break it, change direction
                        floors[floor] = floors[floor][:next_pos] + '.' + floors[floor][next_pos + 1:]
                        direction = -direction
                    elif floors[floor][next_pos] == '#':
                        # Concrete wall - change direction only
                        direction = -direction
        
        seconds += 1
        
        # Check if we reached first floor
        if floor == 0:
            break
    
    print(seconds)

solve()
```

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

Joe has been hurt on the Internet. Now he is storming around the house, destroying everything in his path.

Joe's house has n floors, each floor is a segment of m cells. Each cell either contains nothing (it is an empty cell), or has a brick or a concrete wall (always something one of three). It is believed that each floor is surrounded by a concrete wall on the left and on the right.

Now Joe is on the n-th floor and in the first cell, counting from left to right. At each moment of time, Joe has the direction of his gaze, to the right or to the left (always one direction of the two). Initially, Joe looks to the right.

Joe moves by a particular algorithm. Every second he makes one of the following actions:   If the cell directly under Joe is empty, then Joe falls down. That is, he moves to this cell, the gaze direction is preserved.  Otherwise consider the next cell in the current direction of the gaze.   If the cell is empty, then Joe moves into it, the gaze direction is preserved.  If this cell has bricks, then Joe breaks them with his forehead (the cell becomes empty), and changes the direction of his gaze to the opposite.  If this cell has a concrete wall, then Joe just changes the direction of his gaze to the opposite (concrete can withstand any number of forehead hits).  

Joe calms down as soon as he reaches any cell of the first floor.

The figure below shows an example Joe's movements around the house.

 [Image] 

Determine how many seconds Joe will need to calm down.


-----Input-----

The first line contains two integers n and m (2 ≤ n ≤ 100, 1 ≤ m ≤ 10^4).

Next n lines contain the description of Joe's house. The i-th of these lines contains the description of the (n - i + 1)-th floor of the house — a line that consists of m characters: "." means an empty cell, "+" means bricks and "#" means a concrete wall.

It is guaranteed that the first cell of the n-th floor is empty.


-----Output-----

Print a single number — the number of seconds Joe needs to reach the first floor; or else, print word "Never" (without the quotes), if it can never happen.

Please, do not use the %lld specifier to read or write 64-bit integers in С++. It is preferred to use the cin, cout streams or the %I64d specifier.

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. Joe starts on the n-th floor (bottom floor) at position 1 (leftmost cell)
2. He initially looks to the right
3. Each second, he performs one of these actions:
   - If the cell directly below him is empty, he falls down to that cell (direction unchanged)
   - Otherwise, he looks in his current direction:
     - If the next cell is empty, he moves there (direction unchanged)
     - If the next cell has bricks ("+"), he breaks them (cell becomes empty), changes direction
     - If the next cell has concrete wall ("#"), he changes direction only
4. Joe stops when he reaches any cell on the first floor (top floor)
5. We need to count seconds until he reaches first floor, or output "Never" if impossible

## Key Insights

1. This is a simulation problem with state-dependent behavior
2. State consists of: current floor, current position, current direction
3. We need to detect cycles to avoid infinite loops
4. If Joe never reaches the first floor, we output "Never"

## Algorithm Steps

1. Parse input to get the grid
2. Use BFS or simulation with state tracking
3. For each state (floor, position, direction), simulate one second
4. Track visited states to detect cycles
5. If we reach floor 1, return the number of seconds
6. If we detect a cycle without reaching floor 1, return "Never"

## Implementation Details

- The grid is given from bottom to top (n-th line = 1st floor)
- We start at floor n, position 0 (0-indexed), direction = right (= 0)
- We need to simulate step by step until we reach floor 0 (first floor)

```python
from collections import deque

def solve():
    n, m = map(int, input().split())
    
    # Read the grid - n lines, where line i represents floor (n-i+1)
    # So line 0 is floor n, line n-1 is floor 1 (first floor)
    grid = []
    for _ in range(n):
        grid.append(input().strip())
    
    # We'll simulate Joe's movement
    # State: (floor, position, direction)
    # direction: 0 = right, 1 = left
    # We start at floor n-1 (0-indexed), position 0, direction 0 (right)
    
    # Use BFS to avoid cycles and track steps
    # Visited states: (floor, position, direction)
    visited = set()
    queue = deque()
    
    # Initial state
    start_floor = n - 1  # 0-indexed, bottom floor
    start_pos = 0
    start_dir = 0  # 0 = right, 1 = left
    steps = 0
    
    queue.append((start_floor, start_pos, start_dir, steps))
    visited.add((start_floor, start_pos, start_dir))
    
    while queue:
        floor, pos, direction, steps = queue.popleft()
        
        # Check if we reached the first floor (index 0)
        if floor == 0:
            print(steps)
            return
        
        # Try to move
        next_floor = floor
        next_pos = pos
        next_dir = direction
        
        # First: check if we can fall down
        if floor > 0:  # Not on the first floor
            below_pos = pos
            below_cell = grid[floor - 1][below_pos]
            if below_cell == '.':
                # Fall down
                next_floor = floor - 1
                next_pos = pos
                next_dir = direction
            else:
                # Try to move in current direction
                if direction == 0:  # right
                    next_pos = pos + 1
                    if next_pos >= m:
                        # Hit concrete wall on right
                        next_dir = 1  # change to left
                    else:
                        next_cell = grid[floor][next_pos]
                        if next_cell == '.':
                            # Move to empty cell
                            next_pos = pos + 1
                            next_dir = direction
                        elif next_cell == '+':
                            # Break brick, change direction
                            next_pos = pos + 1
                            next_dir = 1 - direction
                            # Mark this cell as empty
                            # We'll handle this when we actually move to that position
                        else:  # next_cell == '#'
                            # Hit concrete wall, change direction only
                            next_dir = 1 - direction
                else:  # direction == 1, left
                    next_pos = pos - 1
                    if next_pos < 0:
                        # Hit concrete wall on left
                        next_dir = 0  # change to right
                    else:
                        next_cell = grid[floor][next_pos]
                        if next_cell == '.':
                            # Move to empty cell
                            next_pos = pos - 1
                            next_dir = direction
                        elif next_cell == '+':
                            # Break brick, change direction
                            next_pos = pos - 1
                            next_dir = 1 - direction
                        else:  # next_cell == '#'
                            # Hit concrete wall, change direction only
                            next_dir = 1 - direction
        
        # Important: we need to update the grid state when we break bricks
        # But we can't modify the original grid, so we need to track the current state
        
        # Create a new state for the next move
        new_state = (next_floor, next_pos, next_dir)
        new_steps = steps + 1
        
        # Check if we've seen this state before
        if new_state not in visited:
            visited.add(new_state)
            queue.append((next_floor, next_pos, next_dir, new_steps))
    
    # If we exit the loop without reaching first floor, it's never
    print("Never")

solve()
```

```python
from collections import deque

def solve():
    n, m = map(int, input().split())
    
    # Read the grid - n lines, where line i represents floor (n-i+1)
    # So line 0 is floor n, line n-1 is floor 1 (first floor)
    grid = []
    for _ in range(n):
        grid.append(input().strip())
    
    # We'll simulate Joe's movement
    # State: (floor, position, direction)
    # direction: 0 = right, 1 = left
    
    # We need to track the state to detect cycles
    # But we also need to track which cells are broken (empty)
    # Since the state space is huge, we need to be careful
    
    # Use BFS with state = (floor, position, direction)
    # For each state, we can generate the next state
    visited = set()
    
    # Initial state: floor n-1 (0-indexed), position 0, direction 0 (right)
    queue = deque()
    queue.append((n-1, 0, 0))  # (floor, position, direction)
    visited.add((n-1, 0, 0))
    
    steps = 0
    
    while queue:
        floor, pos, direction = queue.popleft()
        
        # Check if we reached the first floor (index 0)
        if floor == 0:
            print(steps)
            return
        
        # Move one second
        steps += 1
        
        # Check if we can fall down
        if floor > 0:
            below_cell = grid[floor - 1][pos]
            if below_cell == '.':
                # Fall down to the cell directly below
                new_floor = floor - 1
                new_pos = pos
                new_dir = direction
                new_state = (new_floor, new_pos, new_dir)
                if new_state not in visited:
                    visited.add(new_state)
                    queue.append(new_state)
            else:
                # Try to move in current direction
                if direction == 0:  # right
                    next_pos = pos + 1
                    if next_pos >= m:
                        # Hit right wall
                        new_dir = 1
                        new_state = (floor, pos, 1)
                        if new_state not in visited:
                            visited.add(new_state)
                            queue.append(new_state)
                    else:
                        next_cell = grid[floor][next_pos]

                            if new_state not in visited:
                                visited.add(new_state)
                                queue.append(new_state)
                        elif next_cell == '+':
                            # Break brick and change direction
                            # Make the cell empty for future reference
                            # (we don't actually modify the grid, just know it's empty now)
                            new_pos = next_pos
                            new_dir = 1 - direction
                            new_state = (floor, new_pos, new_dir)
                            if new_state not in visited:
                                visited.add(new_state)
                                queue.append(new_state)
                        else:  # next_cell == '#'
                            # Hit concrete wall, change direction
                            new_dir = 1 - direction
                            new_state = (floor, pos, new_dir)
                            if new_state not in visited:
                                visited.add(new_state)
                                queue.append(new_state)
                else:  # direction == 1, left
                    next_pos = pos - 1
                    if next_pos < 0:
                        # Hit left wall
                        new_dir = 0
                        new_state = (floor, pos, 0)
                        if new_state not in visited:
                            visited.add(new_state)
                            queue.append(new_state)

                        next_cell = grid[floor][next_pos]
```

```python
start_pos = 0
start_dir = 0  # right

queue.append((start_floor, start_pos, start_dir))
visited.add((start_floor, start_pos, start_dir))

steps = 0
found = False

while queue:
    floor, pos, direction = queue.popleft()
    
    # Check if we've reached the first floor
    if floor == 0:
        print(steps)
        found = True
        break
    
    steps += 1
    
    # Check if we can fall down to the floor below
    if floor > 0:
        # Look at the cell directly below current position
        below_cell = grid[floor - 1][pos]
        if below_cell == '.':
            # Fall down
            new_floor = floor - 1
            new_pos = pos
            new_dir = direction
            new_state = (new_floor, new_pos, new_dir)
            if new_state not in visited:
                visited.add(new_state)
                queue.append(new_state)
        else:
            # Can't fall down, so try to move in current direction
            if direction == 0:  # right
                next_pos = pos + 1
                if next_pos >= m:
                    # Hit concrete wall on right
                    new_dir = 1
                    new_state = (floor, pos, new_dir)
                    if new_state not in visited:
                        visited.add(new_state)
                        queue.append(new_state)
                else:
                    next_cell = grid[floor][next_pos]
                    if next_cell == '.':
                        # Move to empty cell
                        new_state = (floor, next_pos, direction)
                        if new_state not in visited:
                            visited.add(new_state)
                            queue.append(new_state)
                    elif next_cell == '+':
                        # Break brick, change direction
                        new_pos = next_pos
                        new_dir = 1 - direction
                        new_state = (floor, new_pos, new_dir)
                        if new_state not in visited:
                            visited.add(new_state)
                            queue.append(new_state)
                    else:  # next_cell == '#'
                        # Hit concrete wall, change direction
                        new_dir = 1 - direction
                        new_state = (floor, pos, new_dir)
                        if new_state not in visited:
                            visited.add(new_state)
                            queue.append(new_state)
            else:  # direction == 1, left
                next_pos = pos - 1
                if next_pos < 0:
                    # Hit concrete wall on left
                    new_dir = 0
                    new_state = (floor, pos, new_dir)
                    if new_state not in visited:
                        visited.add(new_state)
                        queue.append(new_state)
                else:
                    next_cell = grid[floor][next_pos]
                    if next_cell == '.':
                        # Move to empty cell
                        new_state = (floor, next_pos, direction)
                        if new_state not in visited:
                            visited.add(new_state)
                            queue.append(new_state)
                    elif next_cell == '+':
                        # Break brick, change direction
                        new_pos = next_pos
                        new_dir = 1 - direction
                        new_state = (floor, new_pos, new_dir)
                        if new_state not in visited:
                            visited.add(new_state)
                            queue.append(new_state)
                    else:  # next_cell == '#'
                        # Hit concrete wall, change direction
                        new_dir = 1 - direction
                        new_state = (floor, pos, new_dir)
                        if new_state not in visited:
                            visited.add(new_state)
                            queue.append(new_state)

if not found:
    print("Never")
```

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

At the big break Nastya came to the school dining room. There are $n$ pupils in the school, numbered from $1$ to $n$. Unfortunately, Nastya came pretty late, so that all pupils had already stood in the queue, i.e. Nastya took the last place in the queue. Of course, it's a little bit sad for Nastya, but she is not going to despond because some pupils in the queue can agree to change places with some other pupils.

Formally, there are some pairs $u$, $v$ such that if the pupil with number $u$ stands directly in front of the pupil with number $v$, Nastya can ask them and they will change places. 

Nastya asks you to find the maximal number of places in queue she can move forward. 


-----Input-----

The first line contains two integers $n$ and $m$ ($1 \leq n \leq 3 \cdot 10^{5}$, $0 \leq m \leq 5 \cdot 10^{5}$) — the number of pupils in the queue and number of pairs of pupils such that the first one agrees to change places with the second one if the first is directly in front of the second.

The second line contains $n$ integers $p_1$, $p_2$, ..., $p_n$ — the initial arrangement of pupils in the queue, from the queue start to its end ($1 \leq p_i \leq n$, $p$ is a permutation of integers from $1$ to $n$). In other words, $p_i$ is the number of the pupil who stands on the $i$-th position in the queue.

The $i$-th of the following $m$ lines contains two integers $u_i$, $v_i$ ($1 \leq u_i, v_i \leq n, u_i \neq v_i$), denoting that the pupil with number $u_i$ agrees to change places with the pupil with number $v_i$ if $u_i$ is directly in front of $v_i$. It is guaranteed that if $i \neq j$, than $v_i \neq v_j$ or $u_i \neq u_j$. Note that it is possible that in some pairs both pupils agree to change places with each other.

Nastya is the last person in the queue, i.e. the pupil with number $p_n$.


-----Output-----

Print a single integer — the number of places in queue she can move forward.


-----Examples-----
Input
2 1
1 2
1 2

Output
1
Input
3 3
3 1 2
1 2
3 1
3 2

Output
2
Input
5 2
3 1 5 4 2
5 2
5 4

Output
1


-----Note-----

In the first example Nastya can just change places with the first pupil in the queue.

Optimal sequence of changes in the second example is   change places for pupils with numbers $1$ and $3$.  change places for pupils with numbers $3$ and $2$.  change places for pupils with numbers $1$ and $2$. 

The queue looks like $[3, 1, 2]$, then $[1, 3, 2]$, then $[1, 2, 3]$, and finally $[2, 1, 3]$ after these operations.

The input will be stdin and you should print your solution to stdout

After reading similar problems, I realize this is about finding the longest path that can be formed through valid swaps to move Nastya forward.

## Final Algorithm

1. Build a graph of valid swaps (undirected, since if u can swap with v when u is in front, we can consider v can swap with u in the reversed position)
2. Use BFS/DFS to find all pupils reachable from Nastya's pupil
3. Among all reachable pupils, find the one that is at the earliest position (smallest index), and that gives us the maximum movement distance

Wait, no. The movement distance is just the number of positions she moves forward. Since she starts at position n-1, if she moves to position k, she moves (n-1-k) positions forward.

So we need to find the minimum position among all reachable pupils through valid swaps, and the answer is (n-1 - min_position).

But the swaps can happen in any order, so if a pupil at position i can swap with Nastya, we can move Nastya to position i.

So the answer is the maximum k such that there's a path from Nastya's pupil to a pupil at position (n-1-k).

Actually, it's simpler: we want to find the maximum number of positions Nastya can move forward, which is equivalent to finding the smallest position index that can be reached by a valid swap chain from Nastya.

So steps:
1. Build graph of valid swaps
2. BFS/DFS from Nastya's pupil to find all reachable pupils
3. Among the reachable pupils, find the minimum index (position) that can be reached
4. Answer = (n-1) - min_index

```python
from collections import defaultdict, deque

n, m = map(int, input().split())
p = list(map(int, input().split()))

# Build graph of valid swaps
graph = defaultdict(set)

# Read m pairs
for _ in range(m):
    u, v = map(int, input().split())
    graph[u].add(v)
    graph[v].add(u)  # bidirectional for swap possibility

# Nastya is at position n-1 with pupil p[n-1]
nastya_pupil = p[n-1]
nastya_position = n - 1

# BFS to find all reachable pupils from nastya_pupil
visited = set()
queue = deque([nastya_pupil])
visited.add(nastya_pupil)

min_position = n - 1  # worst case, she doesn't move

while queue:
    current_pupil = queue.popleft()
    
    # Check all positions where this pupil currently is
    # We need to find the position of current_pupil in the queue
    # Actually, we don't know the current position of each pupil, only their IDs
    
    # We need to find which positions are reachable
    # Instead, we can find the minimum position among all reachable pupils
    
    # For each pupil we visit, we want to know if it can be at a position that allows Nastya to move forward
    # But we don't have position info in the graph
    
    # Let me reconsider: we need to find the minimum index among all positions where we can have a pupil that can swap with Nastya
    # But we don't know where each pupil is currently
    
    # Actually, we can track position information: when do we get a position?
    pass

# I think I'm overcomplicating this.

# Alternative approach:
# We want to know what positions can be "reached" by Nastya through valid swaps.
# Since swaps only happen when a pupil is directly in front of another, the key is to find the chain of valid swaps.

# Actually, let's think differently: we can use a graph where nodes are positions,
# and edges exist between positions if the pupils at those positions can swap.

# But the swap condition is specified by pupil numbers.

# After reading solutions to similar problems, the correct approach is:
# Build a graph of valid swaps (bidirectional)
# Find all pupils reachable from Nastya's pupil
# Among those pupils, find the one that is at the earliest position (smallest index)
# Answer = (n-1) - (that position)

# So we need to know the position of each pupil in the initial queue.

# Build mapping from pupil to position
pupil_to_pos = {}
for i in range(n):
    pupil_to_pos[p[i]] = i

# BFS from Nastya's pupil
visited = set()
queue = deque([nastya_pupil])
visited.add(nastya_pupil)

min_reachable_pos = n - 1  # initially, Nastya is at last position

while queue:
    current_pupil = queue.popleft()
    current_pos = pupil_to_pos[current_pupil]
    
    # If we found a pupil at a position earlier than current min, update
    if current_pos < min_reachable_pos:
        min_reachable_pos = current_pos
    
    # Add all neighbors that haven't been visited
    for neighbor in graph[current_pupil]:
        if neighbor not in visited:
            visited.add(neighbor)
            queue.append(neighbor)

# The answer is how many positions Nastya can move forward
answer = (n - 1) - min_reachable_pos
print(answer)
```

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a queue of `n` pupils, with Nastya at the last position (position `n`)
2. We have `m` pairs of pupils `(u, v)` where pupil `u` agrees to swap with pupil `v` if `u` is directly in front of `v`
3. We want to find the maximum number of positions Nastya can move forward in the queue

## Key Insights

1. Nastya starts at position `n` and we want to move her to position `n - k` where `k` is the maximum number of positions she can move forward.
2. A pupil can move forward only if they are directly in front of someone they agree to swap with.
3. This is essentially a graph problem where we need to find connected components of "movable" positions.

## Approach

1. Build a directed graph where an edge from `u` to `v` means pupil `u` can swap with pupil `v` when `u` is in front of `v`.
2. Since swaps are mutual, we need to consider both directions when building the graph.
3. For each pupil that can swap with another, we can potentially move the "chain" of pupils.
4. The key insight is that we want to find the longest path from Nastya's position backward through the swap connections.

Wait, let me reconsider. The problem is about positions, not pupil numbers.

Actually, let's think differently:

- Nastya is at the end, position `n`
- We want to move her forward
- When a pupil `u` is directly in front of pupil `v`, and they agree to swap, then pupil `u` moves to position `v`'s original position, and pupil `v` moves to position `u`'s original position.
- We can chain these swaps to move Nastya forward.

The key insight: We need to find all pupils that can reach Nastya's position through a chain of swaps, and see how far we can move Nastya.

Actually, let's build a graph where:
- Nodes are positions (1 to n)
- An edge from position `i` to position `j` exists if the pupil at position `i` can swap with the pupil at position `j` (where the pupil at `i` is in front of the pupil at `j`)

```python
from collections import defaultdict, deque

n, m = map(int, input().split())
p = list(map(int, input().split()))

# Build graph of pupils
graph = defaultdict(set)

# Read swap pairs
for _ in range(m):
    u, v = map(int, input().split())
    graph[u].add(v)
    graph[v].add(u)

# Find Nastya's pupil
nastya_pupil = p[-1]

# Find all pupils in the same connected component as Nastya
visited = set()
queue = deque([nastya_pupil])
visited.add(nastya_pupil)

component = set()
while queue:
    pupil = queue.popleft()
    component.add(pupil)
    for neighbor in graph[pupil]:
        if neighbor not in visited:
            visited.add(neighbor)
            queue.append(neighbor)

# Now we need to find how far Nastya can move forward
# The key insight is that we can move Nastya forward by the number of positions
# that can be "covered" by the connected component

# Actually, the answer is the maximum distance from the initial position to any position
# in the component, but we need to consider the original arrangement

# We need to find the positions where pupils in the component are located
# and see what's the minimum position we can reach

# For each pupil in the component, we know their initial position
# We can potentially swap them with others, so we can move Nastya to any position
# But we want to know how far forward she can move

# Actually, the answer is simply the length of the longest chain of moves
# that can occur in the connected component

# After reading standard solutions for similar problems, I believe the answer
# is the number of positions that can be "reached" by swapping, which is
# the maximum over all pupils in the component of (initial_position - target_position)

# But I think there's a simpler insight: we can move Nastya forward by k positions
# if there's a sequence of swaps that brings her from position n to position n-k.

# The key is that if the connected component contains pupils at positions that can be 
# rearranged, then Nastya can move forward.

# Nastya's initial position to any other position in the component, considering the 
# original arrangement.

# Nastya starts at position n, so she can move forward by (n - min_position) positions
```

```python
from collections import defaultdict, deque

n, m = map(int, input().split())
p = list(map(int, input().split()))

# Build graph of pupils based on swap agreements
graph = defaultdict(set)

for _ in range(m):
    u, v = map(int, input().split())
    graph[u].add(v)
    graph[v].add(u)

# Find Nastya's pupil (last in the queue)
nastya_pupil = p[-1]

# Find connected component containing Nastya's pupil
visited = set()
queue = deque([nastya_pupil])
visited.add(nastya_pupil)
component = set([nastya_pupil])

while queue:
    pupil = queue.popleft()
    for neighbor in graph[pupil]:
        if neighbor not in visited:
            visited.add(neighbor)
            queue.append(neighbor)
            component.add(neighbor)

# Find the positions of all pupils in the component
positions = {}
for i in range(n):
    positions[p[i]] = i + 1  # 1-indexed position

# Find the minimum position among all pupils in the component
min_position = min(positions[pupil] for pupil in component)

# Nastya starts at position n, so she can move forward by (n - min_position) positions
answer = n - min_position

print(answer)
```

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

At the big break Nastya came to the school dining room. There are $n$ pupils in the school, numbered from $1$ to $n$. Unfortunately, Nastya came pretty late, so that all pupils had already stood in the queue, i.e. Nastya took the last place in the queue. Of course, it's a little bit sad for Nastya, but she is not going to despond because some pupils in the queue can agree to change places with some other pupils.

Formally, there are some pairs $u$, $v$ such that if the pupil with number $u$ stands directly in front of the pupil with number $v$, Nastya can ask them and they will change places. 

Nastya asks you to find the maximal number of places in queue she can move forward. 


-----Input-----

The first line contains two integers $n$ and $m$ ($1 \leq n \leq 3 \cdot 10^{5}$, $0 \leq m \leq 5 \cdot 10^{5}$) — the number of pupils in the queue and number of pairs of pupils such that the first one agrees to change places with the second one if the first is directly in front of the second.

The second line contains $n$ integers $p_1$, $p_2$, ..., $p_n$ — the initial arrangement of pupils in the queue, from the queue start to its end ($1 \leq p_i \leq n$, $p$ is a permutation of integers from $1$ to $n$). In other words, $p_i$ is the number of the pupil who stands on the $i$-th position in the queue.

The $i$-th of the following $m$ lines contains two integers $u_i$, $v_i$ ($1 \leq u_i, v_i \leq n, u_i \neq v_i$), denoting that the pupil with number $u_i$ agrees to change places with the pupil with number $v_i$ if $u_i$ is directly in front of $v_i$. It is guaranteed that if $i \neq j$, than $v_i \neq v_j$ or $u_i \neq u_j$. Note that it is possible that in some pairs both pupils agree to change places with each other.

Nastya is the last person in the queue, i.e. the pupil with number $p_n$.


-----Output-----

Print a single integer — the number of places in queue she can move forward.


-----Examples-----
Input
2 1
1 2
1 2

Output
1
Input
3 3
3 1 2
1 2
3 1
3 2

Output
2
Input
5 2
3 1 5 4 2
5 2
5 4

Output
1


-----Note-----

In the first example Nastya can just change places with the first pupil in the queue.

Optimal sequence of changes in the second example is   change places for pupils with numbers $1$ and $3$.  change places for pupils with numbers $3$ and $2$.  change places for pupils with numbers $1$ and $2$. 

The queue looks like $[3, 1, 2]$, then $[1, 3, 2]$, then $[1, 2, 3]$, and finally $[2, 1, 3]$ after these operations.

The input will be stdin and you should print your solution to stdout


Now solve the problem and return the code.### Format: Read the inputs from stdin solve the problem and write the answer to stdout (do not directly test on the sample inputs). Enclose your code within delimiters as follows. Ensure that when the python program runs, it reads the inputs, runs the algorithm and writes output to STDOUT.
```python
# YOUR CODE HERE
```

```python
def solve():
    n, m = map(int, input().split())
    p = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    # Create union-find structure for pupils
    parent = list(range(n + 1))
    
    def find(x):
        if parent[x] != x:
            parent[x] = find(parent[x])
        return parent[x]
    
    def union(x, y):
        px, py = find(x), find(y)
        if px != py:
            parent[px] = py
    
    # Process swap conditions
    for _ in range(m):
        u, v = map(int, input().split())
        union(u, v)
    
    # Find which component Nastya is in
    nastya_pupil = p[-1]  # last pupil in the queue
    nastya_component = find(nastya_pupil)
    
    # Count how far Nastya can move forward
    max_forward = 0
    for i in range(n - 2, -1, -1):  # from n-2 down to 0
        pupil_at_i = p[i]
        if find(pupil_at_i) == nastya_component:
            # Nastya can move to position i
            forward_positions = (n - 1 - i)
            max_forward = max(max_forward, forward_positions)
    
    print(max_forward)

solve()
```

def solve():
    n, m = map(int, input().split())
    p = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    # Create union-find structure for pupils
    parent = list(range(n + 1))
    
    def find(x):
        if parent[x] != x:
            parent[x] = find(parent[x])
        return parent[x]
    
    def union(x, y):
        px, py = find(x), find(y)
        if px != py:
            parent[px] = py
    
    # Process swap conditions
    for _ in range(m):
        u, v = map(int, input().split())
        union(u, v)
    
    # Find which component Nastya is in
    nastya_pupil = p[-1]  # last pupil in the queue
    nastya_component = find(nastya_pupil)
    
    # Count how far Nastya can move forward
    max_forward = 0
    for i in range(n - 2, -1, -1):  # from n-2 down to 0
        pupil_at_i = p[i]
        if find(pupil_at_i) == nastya_component:
            # Nastya can move to position i
            forward_positions = (n - 1 - i)
            max_forward = max(max_forward, forward_positions)
    
    print(max_forward)

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

At the big break Nastya came to the school dining room. There are $n$ pupils in the school, numbered from $1$ to $n$. Unfortunately, Nastya came pretty late, so that all pupils had already stood in the queue, i.e. Nastya took the last place in the queue. Of course, it's a little bit sad for Nastya, but she is not going to despond because some pupils in the queue can agree to change places with some other pupils.

Formally, there are some pairs $u$, $v$ such that if the pupil with number $u$ stands directly in front of the pupil with number $v$, Nastya can ask them and they will change places. 

Nastya asks you to find the maximal number of places in queue she can move forward. 


-----Input-----

The first line contains two integers $n$ and $m$ ($1 \leq n \leq 3 \cdot 10^{5}$, $0 \leq m \leq 5 \cdot 10^{5}$) — the number of pupils in the queue and number of pairs of pupils such that the first one agrees to change places with the second one if the first is directly in front of the second.

The second line contains $n$ integers $p_1$, $p_2$, ..., $p_n$ — the initial arrangement of pupils in the queue, from the queue start to its end ($1 \leq p_i \leq n$, $p$ is a permutation of integers from $1$ to $n$). In other words, $p_i$ is the number of the pupil who stands on the $i$-th position in the queue.

The $i$-th of the following $m$ lines contains two integers $u_i$, $v_i$ ($1 \leq u_i, v_i \leq n, u_i \neq v_i$), denoting that the pupil with number $u_i$ agrees to change places with the pupil with number $v_i$ if $u_i$ is directly in front of $v_i$. It is guaranteed that if $i \neq j$, than $v_i \neq v_j$ or $u_i \neq u_j$. Note that it is possible that in some pairs both pupils agree to change places with each other.

Nastya is the last person in the queue, i.e. the pupil with number $p_n$.


-----Output-----

Print a single integer — the number of places in queue she can move forward.


-----Examples-----
Input
2 1
1 2
1 2

Output
1
Input
3 3
3 1 2
1 2
3 1
3 2

Output
2
Input
5 2
3 1 5 4 2
5 2
5 4

Output
1


-----Note-----

In the first example Nastya can just change places with the first pupil in the queue.

Optimal sequence of changes in the second example is   change places for pupils with numbers $1$ and $3$.  change places for pupils with numbers $3$ and $2$.  change places for pupils with numbers $1$ and $2$. 

The queue looks like $[3, 1, 2]$, then $[1, 3, 2]$, then $[1, 2, 3]$, and finally $[2, 1, 3]$ after these operations.

The input will be stdin and you should print your solution to stdout
