View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

After finding and moving to the new planet that supports human life, discussions started on which currency should be used. After long negotiations, Bitcoin was ultimately chosen as the universal currency.

These were the great news for Alice, whose grandfather got into Bitcoin mining in 2013, and accumulated a lot of them throughout the years. Unfortunately, when paying something in bitcoin everyone can see how many bitcoins you have in your public address wallet. 

This worried Alice, so she decided to split her bitcoins among multiple different addresses, so that every address has at most x satoshi (1 bitcoin = 10^8 satoshi). She can create new public address wallets for free and is willing to pay f fee in satoshi per transaction to ensure acceptable speed of transfer. The fee is deducted from the address the transaction was sent from. Tell Alice how much total fee in satoshi she will need to pay to achieve her goal.

Input

First line contains number N (1 ≤ N ≤ 200 000) representing total number of public addresses Alice has.

Next line contains N integer numbers a_i (1 ≤ a_i ≤ 10^9) separated by a single space, representing how many satoshi Alice has in her public addresses.

Last line contains two numbers x, f (1 ≤ f < x ≤ 10^9) representing maximum number of satoshies Alice can have in one address, as well as fee in satoshies she is willing to pay per transaction. 

Output

Output one integer number representing total fee in satoshi Alice will need to pay to achieve her goal.

Example

Input

3
13 7 6
6 2


Output

4

Note

Alice can make two transactions in a following way:

0. 13 7 6 (initial state)

1. 6 7 6 5 (create new address and transfer from first public address 5 satoshies)

2. 6 4 6 5 1 (create new address and transfer from second address 1 satoshi)

Since cost per transaction is 2 satoshies, total fee is 4.

The input will be given via stdin and the output should be printed to stdout by your code.

Enclose your code within delimiters as follows. Ensure that when the python program runs, it reads the inputs, runs the algorithm and writes output to STDOUT.
```python
# YOUR CODE HERE
```

## Problem Understanding

1. Alice has N public addresses with various amounts of satoshis
2. She wants to split her bitcoins so that each address has at most x satoshis
3. Each transaction costs f satoshis (deducted from the source address)
4. We need to find the total fee she will pay

## Key Insights

1. For each address with `a_i > x`, we need to split it into multiple addresses
2. When we transfer money from an address, we pay a fee `f` that is deducted from the source
3. The amount we actually transfer is `(amount - fee)` because the fee is deducted from the source
4. We need to determine how many transactions we need for each address

Rearranging: `a_i - x ≤ k * f`
So `k ≥ (a_i - x) / f`

Since k must be an integer, `k = ceil((a_i - x) / f)`.

But in the example:
- For 13, x=6: excess = 7, f=2, k = ceil(7/2) = 4
- For 7, x=6: excess = 1, f=2, k = ceil(1/2) = 1
- Total fee = (4 + 1) * 2 = 10, but expected is 4.

This is wrong.

Actually, looking at the process:
- We transfer from 13: we transfer 5, cost 2, new amount = 13 - 5 - 2 = 6
- We transfer from 7: we transfer 1, cost 2, new amount = 7 - 1 - 2 = 4

Total fee = 2 + 2 = 4.

The key insight is that for each address with amount `a_i > x`, we need to make `ceil((a_i - x) / f)` transactions? No.

Actually, after each transaction, the source decreases by the amount transferred + fee. But we don't care about the amount transferred, we just care about the fee.

We need to completely remove the excess `(a_i - x)` satoshis. Each transaction allows us to transfer some amount and pay `f` fee.

The minimum number of transactions needed to transfer an amount of `excess` is at least `ceil(excess / max_transfer)`. But there's no upper limit on max_transfer.

Actually, we can transfer the entire excess in one transaction, as long as after the transaction the source becomes ≤ x.

If we transfer `t` from address `a_i`, the new amount is `a_i - t - f`. We need `a_i - t - f ≤ x`, so `t ≥ a_i - x - f`.

But we also need `t ≥ 0`.

If `a_i - x - f ≥ 0`, we can transfer `a_i - x - f` and the amount becomes `x`.

If `a_i - x - f < 0`, then we can transfer all excess and the amount becomes `a_i - (a_i - x) - f = x - f < x` (which is fine).

Actually, for any `a_i > x`, we can always transfer enough to get down to `x` or below.

The question is: how many transactions do we need?

After k transactions, the amount becomes `a_i - (sum of transfers + k*f)`.

We need this to be ≤ x, so `a_i - (sum of transfers + k*f) ≤ x`.

The sum of transfers can be as large as needed, so the minimum k is when we minimize the transfers, which means we want to maximize the amount transferred in each transaction.

The minimum k is when `(a_i - x) ≤ k * f` (because the fee alone removes k*f amount, and transfers remove the rest).

So `k ≥ (a_i - x) / f`, so `k = ceil((a_i - x) / f)`.

But this gives wrong answer in the example.

Let me check: for 13, x=6, f=2: excess=7, k=ceil(7/2)=4, fee=4*2=8
For 7, x=6, f=2: excess=1, k=ceil(1/2)=1, fee=1*2=2
Total fee=8+2=10, not 4.

I see the mistake. In the example, Alice makes 2 transactions, total fee 4, not 10.

Looking at the note: "transfer from first public address 5 satoshies" and "transfer from second address 1 satoshi".

The key is that the total amount transferred is 6 (5+1), and the total fee is 4.

For each address with amount `a_i > x`, we need to transfer `(a_i - x)` satoshis to new addresses. Each transfer costs `f` fee.

So total fee = (total excess transferred) / 1 * f? No, total fee = (number of transactions) * f.

In the example, total excess = 13-6 + 7-6 = 7+1 = 8, but total fee is 4.

Actually, total amount before splitting: 13+7+6 = 26
After splitting: 6+4+6+5+1 = 22
Amount transferred = 26-22 = 4

The amount transferred is not equal to total excess.

I think I need to think differently.

After careful analysis and looking at the example, I see:
- We start with amounts: 13, 7, 6
- We want all amounts ≤ 6
- For 13: we need to reduce to 6 or less, so excess 7
- For 7: we need to reduce to 6 or less, so excess 1
- Total excess to be transferred: 7 + 1 = 8

But the example shows we transfer 5 and 1, total 6, leaving 6, 4, 6, 5, 1.

The total amount after is 6+4+6+5+1 = 22
Original amount: 13+7+6 = 26
Amount lost: 4

The amount lost is exactly the total fee.

So: total fee = total amount lost = total original amount - total final amount.

Final amounts: all ≤ x, so final amount ≤ N*x (where N is number of addresses)

But we create new addresses, so the final number of addresses is greater than N.

Let me denote:
- Original sum = S
- Final sum = S - total_fee (since fee is deducted from source)
- Final sum ≤ (number of final addresses) * x

But we can't easily calculate the number of final addresses.

Actually, the total fee is what we lose, and that equals the total amount that couldn't be kept in original addresses.

For each address with amount a_i:
- If a_i ≤ x, it stays as is
- If a_i > x, we need to transfer (a_i - x) amount to other addresses (or to new addresses) and pay fee in the process

But the fee is deducted from the source address.

The key insight from the example: the total fee is the sum of fees for all transactions.

For each address that has excess, we need to make transactions to reduce it to ≤ x.

For an address with amount a_i > x, we need to make k transactions such that after all transactions, the amount is ≤ x.

After k transactions, the amount is a_i - (sum of amounts transferred) - k*f ≤ x.

The sum of amounts transferred can be chosen to be as large as possible, so the minimum k is ceil((a_i - x) / f).

But this gives wrong answer.

Wait, in the example with a_i=13, x=6, f=2:
- We need to reduce from 13 to 6 or less
- We make one transaction: transfer 5, cost 2, new amount = 13 - 5 - 2 = 6
- Only one transaction needed

Similarly for a_i=7, x=6, f=2:
- We make one transaction: transfer 1, cost 2, new amount = 7 - 1 - 2 = 4
- One transaction needed

Total transactions = 2, total fee = 2*2 = 4.

For a_i > x, how many transactions do we need?

We need the final amount ≤ x. After k transactions, the amount is a_i - (sum of transfers) - k*f ≤ x.

The sum of transfers is at least 0, so we need a_i - k*f ≤ x, which gives k ≥ (a_i - x) / f.

So k = ceil((a_i - x) / f).

For a_i=13, x=6, f=2: (13-6)/2 = 7/2 = 3.5, ceil = 4. But we only need 1 transaction.

I see the mistake. The sum of transfers is not bounded by the excess. We can transfer more than (a_i - x) if we want, but we don't need to.

Actually, we can reduce the amount from a_i to x in one transaction by transferring (a_i - x - f) satoshis.

The new amount will be a_i - (a_i - x - f) - f = a_i - a_i + x + f - f = x.

So we can always do it in one transaction if a_i - x - f ≥ 0.

If a_i - x - f < 0, then we can transfer all excess and the new amount will be a_i - (a_i - x) - f = x - f < x, which is still ≤ x.

So in all cases, we can reduce any address with a_i > x to ≤ x in exactly one transaction!

For a_i = 13, x=6, f=2: we transfer 13 - 6 - 2 = 5, new amount = 13 - 5 - 2 = 6.
For a_i = 7, x=6, f=2: we transfer 7 - 6 - 2 = -1, but we can't transfer negative amount.

So we need to transfer at least 0. In this case, transfer 0, new amount = 7 - 0 - 2 = 5 ≤ 6.

For a_i = 7, x=6, f=2: if we transfer 0, new amount = 5 ≤ 6, so one transaction is enough.

For any a_i > x, we can reduce it to ≤ x in one transaction by transferring max(0, a_i - x - f) amount, and the new amount will be a_i - (a_i - x - f) - f = x if a_i > x + f, or a_i - f if a_i ≤ x + f.

In both cases, final amount ≤ x.

Therefore, for each address with a_i > x, we need exactly one transaction to reduce it to ≤ x.

For addresses with a_i ≤ x, no transaction needed.

Total transactions = number of addresses with a_i > x.

Total fee = (number of addresses with a_i > x) * f.

Let me verify with the example:
- Addresses: 13, 7, 6
- a_i > x (x=6): 13 and 7, both > 6
- Number of transactions = 2
- Total fee = 2 * 2 = 4

Yes! This matches.

But what if f is very large? For example, a_i = 10, x = 6, f = 100.
Then a_i - x - f = 10 - 6 - 100 = -96 < 0.
We can transfer 0, new amount = 10 - 0 - 100 = -90, which is negative.

This is a problem.

The fee cannot exceed the amount in the address.

So we need to ensure that after the transaction, the amount is not negative.

For an address with a_i > x, we need to make at least one transaction, and the amount after transaction must be ≥ 0.

After one transaction, new amount = a_i - t - f ≥ 0, where t is the amount transferred.

We also need new amount ≤ x, so a_i - t - f ≤ x, which gives t ≥ a_i - x - f.

But t ≥ 0, so t ≥ max(0, a_i - x - f).

We also need a_i - t - f ≥ 0, so t ≤ a_i - f.

For the transaction to be valid, we need max(0, a_i - x - f) ≤ a_i - f.

This is true because a_i - x - f ≤ a_i - f.

But we also need the final amount to be ≥ 0, so a_i - t - f ≥ 0, which means t ≤ a_i - f.

And we need t ≥ max(0, a_i - x - f).

So we need max(0, a_i - x - f) ≤ a_i - f, which is true.

But we also need the amount to be ≥ 0, so if a_i - f < 0, then we cannot make any transaction from this address.

But the problem states that f < x ≤ 10^9, and a_i ≤ 10^9.

We have f < x, and a_i > x, so a_i ≥ x+1.

Then a_i - f > x - f > 0, since f < x.

So a_i - f > 0, so we can always make the transaction.

Therefore, for each address with a_i > x, we can make exactly one transaction to reduce it to at most x, and the final amount will be ≥ 0.

Therefore, total fee = (number of addresses with a_i > x) * f.

Let me verify with the example and edge cases.

Example: [13,7,6], x=6, f=2
- a_i > 6: 13,7 → count = 2
- fee = 2 * 2 = 4 ✓

Edge case: a_i = x + 1, x = 10, f = 5
- a_i = 11 > 10
- transfer t = max(0, 11-10-5) = max(0, -4) = 0
- new amount = 11 - 0 - 5 = 6 ≤ 10 ✓
- fee = 1 * 5 = 5

Another edge case: a_i = x + f + 1, x = 10, f = 5
- a_i = 16 > 10
- t = max(0, 16-10-5) = 1
- new amount = 16 - 1 - 5 = 10 ≤ 10 ✓
- fee = 1 * 5 = 5

All cases work.

Therefore, the solution is:

Read N
Read array a of N integers
Read x, f

Count the number of elements in a that are greater than x
Total fee = count * f

```python
n = int(input())
a = list(map(int, input().split()))
x, f = map(int, input().split())

count = 0
for amount in a:
    if amount > x:
        count += 1

total_fee = count * f
print(total_fee)
```

You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

After finding and moving to the new planet that supports human life, discussions started on which currency should be used. After long negotiations, Bitcoin was ultimately chosen as the universal currency.

These were the great news for Alice, whose grandfather got into Bitcoin mining in 2013, and accumulated a lot of them throughout the years. Unfortunately, when paying something in bitcoin everyone can see how many bitcoins you have in your public address wallet. 

This worried Alice, so she decided to split her bitcoins among multiple different addresses, so that every address has at most x satoshi (1 bitcoin = 10^8 satoshi). She can create new public address wallets for free and is willing to pay f fee in satoshi per transaction to ensure acceptable speed of transfer. The fee is deducted from the address the transaction was sent from. Tell Alice how much total fee in satoshi she will need to pay to achieve her goal.

Input

First line contains number N (1 ≤ N ≤ 200 000) representing total number of public addresses Alice has.

Next line contains N integer numbers a_i (1 ≤ a_i ≤ 10^9) separated by a single space, representing how many satoshi Alice has in her public addresses.

Last line contains two numbers x, f (1 ≤ f < x ≤ 10^9) representing maximum number of satoshies Alice can have in one address, as well as fee in satoshies she is willing to pay per transaction. 

Output

Output one integer number representing total fee in satoshi Alice will need to pay to achieve her goal.

Example

Input

3
13 7 6
6 2


Output

4

Note

Alice can make two transactions in a following way:

0. 13 7 6 (initial state)

1. 6 7 6 5 (create new address and transfer from first public address 5 satoshies)

2. 6 4 6 5 1 (create new address and transfer from second address 1 satoshi)

Since cost per transaction is 2 satoshies, total fee is 4.

The input will be given via stdin and the output should be printed to stdout by your code.

Now solve the problem by providing the code.### Format: Read the inputs from stdin solve the problem and write the answer to stdout (do not directly test on the sample inputs). ## Problem Understanding

1. Alice has N public addresses with different amounts of satoshis
2. She wants to split her bitcoins so that each address has at most `x` satoshis
3. Each transaction costs `f` satoshis (deducted from the source address)
4. We need to find the total fee she will pay

## Key Insights

1. For each address with amount `a_i` that exceeds `x`, she needs to split it into multiple addresses
2. When splitting an address with amount `a_i` (where `a_i > x`), she needs to create new addresses
3. Each transaction from an address reduces its balance by the amount transferred plus the transaction fee
4. If she transfers `t` satoshis from an address, the address loses `t + f` satoshis (t for the transfer, f for the fee)

## Step-by-Step Solution

- Address 1 has 7 > 6, needs to be split

- Transfer 13 - 6 = 7 satoshis to new address
- But we pay fee f=2, so the transfer amount is 7, fee is 2
- After transfer, original address has 13 - (7 + 2) = 4 satoshis

- Transfer 1 from address 1 to create new address: [6, 4, 6, 5, 1]
- Fee is 2, so address 1 loses 1 + 2 = 3 satoshis, so 7 - 3 = 4 left
- Total fee: 2 + 2 = 4

So the pattern is:
For each address with amount `a_i`:
- If `a_i ≤ x`: no fee needed
- If `a_i > x`: we need to split it
- Each time we create a new address, we transfer some amount and pay fee
- The amount transferred from an address is `(a_i - x)` when we create a new address with exactly `x` satoshis
- But we pay fee `f` per transaction

Actually, let's think differently:
- We can only create as many new addresses as needed
- Each transaction: transfer `t` satoshis from source address, pay fee `f`, so source loses `t + f`
- We want each final address to have at most `x` satoshis

For an address with `a_i > x`:
- We need to reduce it to at most `x`
- The amount that needs to be transferred out is `a_i - x`
- Each transaction transfers some amount and pays fee
- After transferring `t` satoshis, the source address has `a_i - t - f` satoshis
- We need the final balance to be ≤ `x`

Wait, let me simplify:
For each address with amount `a_i > x`, we need to create new addresses until the original address has ≤ `x` left.

The key insight is:
- For each address with amount `a_i > x`, we need to perform transactions until the balance becomes ≤ `x`
- Each transaction reduces the balance by the amount transferred plus the fee
- We can transfer exactly `(a_i - x)` to new addresses in total
- But each transaction costs `f` in fee

Actually, let's think about the total amount that needs to be transferred:
- For address with `a_i > x`, we need to remove `(a_i - x)` satoshis from it
- Each transaction allows us to transfer some amount and pay fee `f`
- The amount transferred from an address is not directly related to the fee

After looking at the example again:
- Address 13: transfers 5 to new address, pays fee 2, so 13 - 5 - 2 = 6
- Address 7: transfers 1 to new address, pays fee 2, so 7 - 1 - 2 = 4

The pattern: for each address `a_i` where `a_i > x`, the amount transferred is `(a_i - x)`, and we pay fee `f` for each such transfer.

But how many transfers do we need? Actually, we just need to make enough transfers so that the source address has ≤ `x`.

- Each transaction transfers some amount and pays fee `f`
- The total amount transferred from this address is `(a_i - x)`
- The total fee paid from this address is the number of transactions × `f`

So for an address with `a_i > x`, we can transfer `(a_i - x)` satoshis to new addresses through multiple transactions. Each transaction pays fee `f`.

The key insight: each time we create a new address and transfer from an existing one, we pay `f` fee. The amount transferred doesn't affect how many fees we pay - we pay one fee per transaction.

For an address with amount `a_i > x`, how many transactions do we need until the balance becomes ≤ `x`?

- Address 7: 7 → 4, transfers 3 satoshis out, pays 2 fee (1 transaction)

But we can make multiple transactions. The minimum number of transactions needed is 1, because we can transfer the entire excess amount in one transaction.

But actually, the problem doesn't require minimizing transactions - we just need to achieve the goal. We can transfer any amount as long as the final balance is ≤ `x`.

For each address with `a_i > x`, we need to transfer at least `a_i - x` satoshis out to new addresses. We can do this in one transaction by transferring exactly `a_i - x` if `a_i - x ≤ a_i - x - f` (which is not possible since `f > 0`).

Wait, let me recalculate: after transferring `t` satoshis and paying fee `f`, the source has `a_i - t - f`.
We want `a_i - t - f ≤ x`, so `t ≥ a_i - x - f`.

For the minimum number of transactions, we can transfer the required amount in one go.

Actually, I think I'm overcomplicating. 

Looking at the example: 
- Address 13: 13 > 6, so needs reduction
- We can create a new address with 6 satoshis, transfer 5 satoshis (since 13-5=8, then minus fee 2 gives 6)
- So transfer amount = 13 - 6 - 2 = 5
- This transfers 5 satoshis to new address
- We pay fee 2
- Total transfers: 5 satoshis

- Address 7: 7 > 6, create new address with 6 satoshis, transfer 1 satoshi (7-1=6, minus fee 2 gives 4)
- Transfer amount = 7 - 6 - 2 = 1
- Pay fee 2

Total fee: 2 + 2 = 4.

So for each address with `a_i > x`:
- We transfer `a_i - x - f` satoshis to new addresses
- We pay fee `f` for this transaction
- The number of transactions is 1 per address where `a_i > x`

Therefore, total fee = number of addresses with `a_i > x` × `f`

Wait, in the example: 2 addresses > 6, fee = 2 × 2 = 4. This matches!

Let me verify with the logic:
- For address with `a_i > x`: we need to reduce it to ≤ `x`
- We make one transaction: transfer some amount, pay fee `f`
- After transaction, remaining balance = `a_i - t - f`
- We want `a_i - t - f ≤ x`, so `t ≥ a_i - x - f`
- We can choose `t = a_i - x - f`, then remaining balance = `a_i - (a_i - x - f) - f = x`
- This works!

And we only need one transaction per address exceeding `x`.

Therefore, the answer is: `f * (number of addresses with a_i > x)`

## Code Implementation

```python
n = int(input())
addresses = list(map(int, input().split()))
x, f = map(int, input().split())

# Count how many addresses have more than x satoshis
count_exceeding = 0
for amount in addresses:
    if amount > x:
        count_exceeding += 1

total_fee = count_exceeding * f
print(total_fee)
```

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. Alice has N public addresses with different amounts of satoshis
2. She wants to split her bitcoins so that each address has at most `x` satoshis
3. Each transaction costs `f` satoshis (deducted from the source address)
4. We need to find the total fee she will pay

## Key Insights

1. For each address with more than `x` satoshis, we need to transfer excess amount to new addresses
2. Each transfer operation costs `f` satoshis (which is deducted from the source address)
3. The amount transferred from an address is `(original_amount - x)` for addresses above `x`
4. For each such transfer, we pay `f` fee, so total fee = number_of_transfers × f

## Step-by-Step Solution

1. **Identify addresses that need splitting**: Any address with amount > x needs to be split
2. **Calculate how much needs to be transferred**: For each address with amount > x, transfer (amount - x) satoshis to new addresses
3. **Count the number of transfers needed**: Each time we move money from an address, it's one transaction
4. **Calculate total fee**: number_of_transfers × fee_per_transaction

Wait, let me reconsider. The key insight is:
- When we transfer money from an address, we send (amount - x) amount to new addresses

## Solution

```python
n = int(input())
addresses = list(map(int, input().split()))
x, f = map(int, input().split())

# Count number of addresses with amount > x
transfers_needed = 0
for amount in addresses:
    if amount > x:
        transfers_needed += 1

total_fee = transfers_needed * f
print(total_fee)
```

After finding and moving to the new planet that supports human life, discussions started on which currency should be used. After long negotiations, Bitcoin was ultimately chosen as the universal currency.

She can create new public address wallets for free and is willing to pay f fee in satoshi per transaction to ensure acceptable speed of transfer. The fee is deducted from the address the transaction was sent from. Tell Alice how much total fee in satoshi she will need to pay to achieve her goal.

Input

First line contains number N (1 ≤ N ≤ 200 000) representing total number of public addresses Alice has.

Next line contains N integer numbers a_i (1 ≤ a_i ≤ 10^9) separated by a single space, representing how many satoshi Alice has in her public addresses.

Last line contains two numbers x, f (1 ≤ f < x ≤ 10^9) representing maximum number of satoshies Alice can have in one address, as well as fee in satoshies she is willing to pay per transaction. 

Output

Output one integer number representing total fee in satoshi Alice will need to pay to achieve her goal.

Example

Input

3
13 7 6
6 2


Output

4

Note

Alice can make two transactions in a following way:

0. 13 7 6 (initial state)

1. 6 7 6 5 (create new address and transfer from first public address 5 satoshies)

2. 6 4 6 5 1 (create new address and transfer from second address 1 satoshi)

Since cost per transaction is 2 satoshies, total fee is 4.

For a source address of a_i > x, after one transaction, it becomes a_i - t - f, where 0 ≤ t ≤ x.

We want a_i - t - f ≤ x, so t ≥ a_i - x - f.

Since t ≤ x, we need a_i - x - f ≤ x, which means a_i ≤ 2x + f.

Since f < x ≤ 10^9, and a_i ≤ 10^9, this is generally true.

The minimum number of transactions to reduce a_i to at most x is: if a_i > x, then we need only one transaction (because we can set t large enough to bring it down).

But in the example with 13 and x=6, f=2:
- a_i = 13, x = 6
- After one transaction: we can transfer t such that 13 - t - 2 ≤ 6, so t ≥ 5
- We can transfer t = 5, then new address gets 5 ≤ 6, source becomes 13 - 5 - 2 = 6 ≤ 6
- Only 1 transaction needed

Similarly for 7: 7 - t - 2 ≤ 6, so t ≥ 1. Transfer t=1, source becomes 4 ≤ 6.

So total transactions = 2, total fee = 2 * 2 = 4.

This matches the expected output.

But is one transaction always sufficient for any a_i > x?

Yes, because we can always transfer t = max(0, a_i - x - f) to ensure the source becomes at most x.

Since a_i > x, a_i - x > 0, and we need t ≥ a_i - x - f.

We also need t ≤ x to ensure the new address gets at most x satoshis.

We need a_i - x - f ≤ x, which means a_i ≤ 2x + f.

Given the constraints 1 ≤ f < x ≤ 10^9, and a_i ≤ 10^9, is a_i ≤ 2x + f always true?

Not necessarily. For example, if a_i is very large, say a_i = 2x + f + 1, then a_i - x - f = x + 1 > x, so we cannot transfer enough to bring the source to ≤ x in one transaction.

In that case, we'd need multiple transactions.

For large a_i, we need multiple transactions.

Let me analyze:

For an address with amount a_i > x:
- We want to reduce it to at most x
- Each transaction reduces it by (t + f) where t ≤ x (to not exceed x in new address)
- The minimum reduction per transaction is f (when t=0), but we can do better by transferring t ≥ a_i - x - f

The maximum reduction per transaction is x + f (when t = x).

So the number of transactions needed is the smallest k such that k * (x + f) ≥ a_i - x.

This means k ≥ (a_i - x) / (x + f)

So k = ceil((a_i - x) / (x + f))

Therefore, for each address with a_i > x, the number of transactions needed is ceil((a_i - x) / (x + f)).

Let me verify with the example:
- Address 13: a_i = 13, x = 6, f = 2
- a_i - x = 7
- x + f = 8
- ceil(7/8) = 1
- Address 7: a_i = 7, x = 6, f = 2
- a_i - x = 1
- x + f = 8
- ceil(1/8) = 1
- Total transactions = 1 + 1 = 2
- Total fee = 2 * 2 = 4 ✓

This matches!

Final algorithm:
1. Read N, array a, and x, f
2. For each address a_i:
   - If a_i > x, add ceil((a_i - x) / (x + f)) to total transactions
3. Total fee = total_transactions * f

```python
import math

# Read inputs
n = int(input())
a = list(map(int, input().split()))
x, f = map(int, input().split())

total_transactions = 0

for amount in a:
    if amount > x:
        excess = amount - x
        # Number of transactions needed to reduce excess
        # Each transaction can reduce by at most (x + f) 
        # We need to reduce by at least 'excess'
        transactions_needed = math.ceil(excess / (x + f))
        total_transactions += transactions_needed

total_fee = total_transactions * f
print(total_fee)
```

        excess = amount - x

        # Each transaction can reduce by at most (x + f) 
        # We need to reduce by at least 'excess'
        transactions_needed = math.ceil(excess / (x + f))
        total_transactions += transactions_needed

total_fee = total_transactions * f
print(total_fee)

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. Alice has N public addresses with various amounts of satoshis
2. She wants to split her bitcoins so that each address has at most `x` satoshis
3. Each transaction costs `f` satoshis (deducted from the source address)
4. She can create new addresses for free
5. We need to find the total fee she'll pay

## Key Insights

1. For each address with amount `a_i` that exceeds `x`, we need to split it
2. If `a_i > x`, we need to transfer the excess amount to create new addresses
3. Each transfer operation costs `f` satoshis, which is deducted from the original address
4. The excess amount to transfer from address `i` is `a_i - x`
5. However, when we transfer money, we pay a fee of `f` satoshis per transaction, so the net amount transferred is `amount - f`

## Algorithm

For each address with amount `a_i`:
- If `a_i <= x`: no action needed
- If `a_i > x`: we need to split it
- The excess is `a_i - x`
- We need to create new addresses to hold this excess

Since `t >= 0`, we need `t >= max(0, a_i - x - f)`.

And `t <= a_i - f`.

We can always choose `t` in this range as long as it's non-empty.

The range is non-empty if `max(0, a_i - x - f) <= a_i - f`, which is always true.

So for any `a_i > x`, we can always reduce it to at most `x` in one transaction.

Therefore, total transactions needed = number of addresses with `a_i > x`.

Total fee = (number of addresses with `a_i > x`) * f.

Let me verify with the example:
- a_i = 13: 13 > 6, so one transaction
- a_i = 7: 7 > 6, so one transaction  
- a_i = 6: 6 ≤ 6, so no transaction
- Total transactions = 2
- Total fee = 2 * 2 = 4 ✓

This matches.

## Solution

```python
n = int(input())
addresses = list(map(int, input().split()))
x, f = map(int, input().split())

# Count addresses with more than x satoshis
transactions = 0
for amount in addresses:
    if amount > x:
        transactions += 1

total_fee = transactions * f
print(total_fee)
```

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

In the year 2xxx, an expedition team landing on a planet found strange objects made by an ancient species living on that planet. They are transparent boxes containing opaque solid spheres (Figure 1). There are also many lithographs which seem to contain positions and radiuses of spheres.

<image>

Figure 1: A strange object

Initially their objective was unknown, but Professor Zambendorf found the cross section formed by a horizontal plane plays an important role. For example, the cross section of an object changes as in Figure 2 by sliding the plane from bottom to top.

<image>

Figure 2: Cross sections at different positions

He eventually found that some information is expressed by the transition of the number of connected figures in the cross section, where each connected figure is a union of discs intersecting or touching each other, and each disc is a cross section of the corresponding solid sphere. For instance, in Figure 2, whose geometry is described in the first sample dataset later, the number of connected figures changes as 0, 1, 2, 1, 2, 3, 2, 1, and 0, at z = 0.0000, 162.0000, 167.0000, 173.0004, 185.0000, 191.9996, 198.0000, 203.0000, and 205.0000, respectively. By assigning 1 for increment and 0 for decrement, the transitions of this sequence can be expressed by an 8-bit binary number 11011000.

For helping further analysis, write a program to determine the transitions when sliding the horizontal plane from bottom (z = 0) to top (z = 36000).



Input

The input consists of a series of datasets. Each dataset begins with a line containing a positive integer, which indicates the number of spheres N in the dataset. It is followed by N lines describing the centers and radiuses of the spheres. Each of the N lines has four positive integers Xi, Yi, Zi, and Ri (i = 1, . . . , N) describing the center and the radius of the i-th sphere, respectively.

You may assume 1 ≤ N ≤ 100, 1 ≤ Ri ≤ 2000, 0 < Xi - Ri < Xi + Ri < 4000, 0 < Yi - Ri < Yi + Ri < 16000, and 0 < Zi - Ri < Zi + Ri < 36000. Each solid sphere is defined as the set of all points (x, y, z) satisfying (x - Xi)2 + (y - Yi)2 + (z - Zi)2 ≤ Ri2.

A sphere may contain other spheres. No two spheres are mutually tangent. Every Zi ± Ri and minimum/maximum z coordinates of a circle formed by the intersection of any two spheres differ from each other by at least 0.01.

The end of the input is indicated by a line with one zero.

Output

For each dataset, your program should output two lines. The first line should contain an integer M indicating the number of transitions. The second line should contain an M-bit binary number that expresses the transitions of the number of connected figures as specified above.

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have transparent boxes containing opaque solid spheres in 3D space
2. We're looking at cross-sections of these spheres at different z-heights (horizontal planes)
3. Each cross-section creates circles (discs) in the plane
4. Connected figures are unions of circles that intersect or touch each other
5. We need to find when the number of connected components changes as we slide the plane from z=0 to z=36000
6. For each transition (increment or decrement), we record 1 for increment, 0 for decrement
7. We output the total number of transitions and the binary string

## Key Insights

1. At any height z, each sphere creates a circle in the z=constant plane
2. The circle's radius is √(R² - (z - Zᵢ)²) when |z - Zᵢ| < R
3. Two circles connect if they intersect or touch
4. The number of connected components changes only at specific z-values where:
   - A sphere starts to appear (z = Zᵢ - R)
   - A sphere disappears (z = Zᵢ + R)
   - Two spheres' circles touch (intersection points at z)
5. We need to find all critical z-values and check the connected components at each

## Algorithm Steps

1. Find all critical z-values (sphere boundaries and intersection points)
2. For each critical z-value, compute the connected components of circles
3. Track the number of components and determine transitions (increment/decrement)
4. Generate the binary string

## Detailed Implementation

```python
import math
from collections import defaultdict

def get_circle_radius(sphere_center, z):
    """Get radius of circle at height z for a sphere"""
    x, y, z_center, r = sphere_center
    dz = abs(z - z_center)
    if dz >= r:
        return 0
    return math.sqrt(r*r - dz*dz)

def get_intersection_z(sphere1, sphere2):
    """Find z-values where two spheres intersect"""
    x1, y1, z1, r1 = sphere1
    x2, y2, z2, r2 = sphere2
    
    # Two spheres intersect if distance between centers is <= r1 + r2
    dist_z = abs(z1 - z2)
    dist_xy = math.sqrt((x1-x2)**2 + (y1-y2)**2)
    dist = math.sqrt(dist_xy**2 + dist_z**2)
    
    if dist > r1 + r2:
        return []  # No intersection
    
    # Check if they intersect in the z-dimension
    # The intersection occurs at z values where the distance in xy-plane is consistent
    
    # For intersection in z-direction, we solve for z such that:
    # (x1-x2)^2 + (y1-y2)^2 + (z-z1)^2 + (z-z2)^2 = r1^2 + r2^2
    # This gives a quadratic in z
    
    # The equation: 2(z1+z2-z)(z-z1) + (z-z1)^2 + (z-z2)^2 = r1^2 + r2^2
    # Actually: distance^2 = (x1-x2)^2 + (y1-y2)^2 + (z-z1)^2 + (z-z2)^2
    
    # (z-z1)^2 + (z-z2)^2 = r1^2 + r2^2 - (x1-x2)^2 - (y1-y2)^2
    # 2z^2 - 2z(z1+z2) + z1^2 + z2^2 = r1^2 + r2^2 - (x1-x2)^2 - (y1-y2)^2
    
    # We only care about z-values where intersection occurs and |z-z1| < r1, |z-z2| < r2
    # We need to solve: (z-z1)^2 + (z-z2)^2 = r1^2 + r2^2 - dist_xy^2
    
    diff_z = z1 - z2
    dist_sq_xy = (x1-x2)**2 + (y1-y2)**2
    
    # We need r1^2 + r2^2 - dist_sq_xy >= 0 for intersection
    if r1*r1 + r2*r2 - dist_sq_xy < 0:
        return []
    
    # The equation for z: 2z^2 - 2z(z1+z2) + (z1^2 + z2^2) = r1^2 + r2^2 - dist_sq_xy
    # 2z^2 - 2z(z1+z2) + (z1^2 + z2^2 - r1^2 - r2^2 + dist_sq_xy) = 0
    
    a = 2.0
    b = -2.0 * (z1 + z2)
    c = z1*z1 + z2*z2 - r1*r1 - r2*r2 + dist_sq_xy
    
    discriminant = b*b - 4*a*c
    if discriminant < 0:
        return []
    
    sqrt_disc = math.sqrt(discriminant)
    z1_intersect = (-b + sqrt_disc) / (2*a)
    z2_intersect = (-b - sqrt_disc) / (2*a)
    
    # Check if these z-values are valid (within the spheres' z-ranges)
    valid_z = []
    
    for z_val in [z1_intersect, z2_intersect]:
        if z_val >= z1 - r1 and z_val <= z1 + r1 and z_val >= z2 - r2 and z_val <= z2 + r2:
            valid_z.append(z_val)
    
    return sorted(valid_z)

def get_connected_components(circles):
    """Get number of connected components in a list of circles"""
    if not circles:
        return 0
    
    # Group circles by their essential parameters - we only care about intersection
    # Two circles intersect if their distance is <= sum of radii
    n = len(circles)
    components = 0
    
    # Use union-find or simple connectivity check
    visited = [False] * n
    components = 0
    
    for i in range(n):
        if not visited[i]:
            components += 1
            # DFS/BFS to mark all connected circles
            stack = [i]
            visited[i] = True
            while stack:
                curr = stack.pop()
                # Check all other circles
                for j in range(n):
                    if not visited[j]:
                        # Distance between circles (in 2D plane)
                        x1, y1, r1 = circles[curr]
                        x2, y2, r2 = circles[j]
                        dist = math.sqrt((x1-x2)**2 + (y1-y2)**2)
                        if dist <= r1 + r2:
                            visited[j] = True
                            stack.append(j)
    
    return components

def solve_dataset(spheres):
    # Get all critical z-values
    critical_z = set()
    
    # Add sphere start and end z-values
    for x, y, z, r in spheres:
        critical_z.add(z - r)
        critical_z.add(z + r)
    
    # Add intersection z-values
    for i in range(len(spheres)):
        for j in range(i + 1, len(spheres)):
            inters = get_intersection_z(spheres[i], spheres[j])
            for z in inters:
                critical_z.add(z)
    
    # Sort critical z-values
    critical_z = sorted(critical_z)
    
    # Check the connected components at each critical point
    transitions = []
    
    # Check all critical points to see transitions
    z_values = [0.0] + critical_z + [36000.0]
    
    # We need to check the number of components between each critical point
    # and see if it changes
    prev_components = 0
    prev_z = 0.0
    
    # We need to check the components at each critical point
    # and determine if it's an increment or decrement
    
    # Prepare circles at each z
    for z in critical_z:
        circles = []
        for x, y, z_center, r in spheres:
            radius = get_circle_radius((x, y, z_center, r), z)
            if radius > 0:
                circles.append((x, y, radius))
        
        components = get_connected_components(circles)
        
        # Check transition from previous position
        if z > 0:  # Handle case where z=0 might be first
            # We need to compare with previous configuration
            # But we don't have full history - we need to check the differences
            pass
    
    # We need a different approach - we'll check at each critical point
    # and compare with the immediately previous critical point
    
    # Actually, we can just check the components at critical points and see changes
    critical_z_list = sorted(critical_z)
    
    components_at_z = []
    for z in critical_z_list:
        circles = []
        for x, y, z_center, r in spheres:
            radius = get_circle_radius((x, y, z_center, r), z)
            if radius > 0:
                circles.append((x, y, radius))
        
        components = get_connected_components(circles)
        components_at_z.append((z, components))
    
    # Now check for transitions between consecutive points
    transitions_bits = []
    for i in range(1, len(components_at_z)):
        z1, comp1 = components_at_z[i-1]
        z2, comp2 = components_at_z[i]
        
        # If comp2 > comp1, it's an increment (1)
        # If comp2 < comp1, it's a decrement (0)
        if comp2 > comp1:
            transitions_bits.append('1')
        else:
            transitions_bits.append('0')
    
    # Also need to check if there are transitions at z=0 and other boundaries
    # But the problem says start at z=0, so we need to include initial state
    
    # The transitions only occur at the critical points
    # We need to output the binary string of transitions
    
    return ''.join(transitions_bits)

def main():
    while True:
        n = int(input().strip())
        if n == 0:
            break
            
        spheres = []
        for _ in range(n):
            x, y, z, r = map(int, input().split())
            spheres.append((x, y, z, r))
        
        # Solve the dataset
        result = solve_dataset(spheres)
        
        # Output
        m = len(result)
        print(m)
        print(result)

if __name__ == "__main__":
```

```python
import math
from collections import defaultdict

def get_circle_radius(sphere_center, z):
    """Get radius of circle at height z for a sphere"""
    x, y, z_center, r = sphere_center
    dz = abs(z - z_center)
    if dz >= r:
        return 0
    return math.sqrt(r*r - dz*dz)

def get_intersection_z(sphere1, sphere2):
    """Find z-values where two spheres intersect"""
    x1, y1, z1, r1 = sphere1
    x2, y2, z2, r2 = sphere2
    
    # Distance between centers in xy-plane
    dist_xy = math.sqrt((x1-x2)**2 + (y1-y2)**2)
    
    # Distance between centers in z-direction
    dist_z = abs(z1 - z2)
    
    # For intersection, total distance must be <= r1 + r2
    if dist_xy + dist_z > r1 + r2:
        return []
    
    # The intersection occurs when the sum of squares of distances equals sum of squares of radii
    # We need to solve: (x1-x2)^2 + (y1-y2)^2 + (z-z1)^2 + (z-z2)^2 = r1^2 + r2^2
    
    # This gives: 2z^2 - 2z(z1+z2) + (z1^2 + z2^2 + dist_xy^2) = r1^2 + r2^2
    # Wait, correction: (z-z1)^2 + (z-z2)^2 = (z-z1)^2 + (z-z2)^2
    # = z^2 - 2z*z1 + z1^2 + z^2 - 2z*z2 + z2^2 = 2z^2 - 2z(z1+z2) + z1^2 + z2^2
    
    # So: 2z^2 - 2z(z1+z2) + z1^2 + z2^2 = r1^2 + r2^2 - dist_xy^2
    # 2z^2 - 2z(z1+z2) + (z1^2 + z2^2 - r1^2 - r2^2 + dist_xy^2) = 0
    
    a = 2.0
    b = -2.0 * (z1 + z2)
    c = z1*z1 + z2*z2 - r1*r1 - r2*r2 + dist_xy*dist_xy
    
    discriminant = b*b - 4*a*c
    if discriminant < 0:
        return []
    
    sqrt_disc = math.sqrt(discriminant)
    z1_intersect = (-b + sqrt_disc) / (2*a)
    z2_intersect = (-b - sqrt_disc) / (2*a)
    
    # Check valid intersection points within both spheres
    valid_z = []
    
    for z_val in [z1_intersect, z2_intersect]:
        # Check if z_val is within both spheres' z-ranges
        if z_val >= z1 - r1 and z_val <= z1 + r1 and z_val >= z2 - r2 and z_val <= z2 + r2:
            valid_z.append(z_val)
    
    return sorted(valid_z)

def get_connected_components(circles):
    """Get number of connected components in a list of circles"""
    if not circles:
        return 0
    
    # Minimum radius for a circle to exist
    n = len(circles)
    visited = [False] * n
    components = 0
    
    for i in range(n):
        if not visited[i]:
            components += 1
            # Use DFS to mark all connected circles
            stack = [i]
            visited[i] = True
            while stack:
                curr = stack.pop()
                # Check all other circles
                for j in range(n):
                    if not visited[j]:
                        x1, y1, r1 = circles[curr]
                        x2, y2, r2 = circles[j]
                        dist = math.sqrt((x1-x2)**2 + (y1-y2)**2)
                        # If distance <= sum of radii, they are connected
                        if dist <= r1 + r2:
                            visited[j] = True
                            stack.append(j)
    
    return components

def main():
    while True:
        line = input().strip()
        n = int(line)
        if n == 0:
            break
            
        spheres = []
        for _ in range(n):
            x, y, z, r = map(int, input().split())
            spheres.append((x, y, z, r))
        
        # Get all critical z-values
        critical_z = set()
        
        # Add sphere boundaries
        for x, y, z, r in spheres:
            critical_z.add(z - r)
            critical_z.add(z + r)
        
        # Add intersection points
        for i in range(len(spheres)):
            for j in range(i + 1, len(spheres)):
                inters = get_intersection_z(spheres[i], spheres[j])
                for z in inters:
                    critical_z.add(z)
        
        # Convert to sorted list
        critical_z_list = sorted(critical_z)
        
        # Check the state at each critical point
        transitions = []
        
        # We need to check transitions between consecutive critical points
        for i in range(1, len(critical_z_list)):
            z_prev = critical_z_list[i-1]
            z_curr = critical_z_list[i]
            
            # Get circles at previous position
            prev_circles = []
            for x, y, z_center, r in spheres:
                radius = get_circle_radius((x, y, z_center, r), z_prev)
                if radius > 0:
                    prev_circles.append((x, y, radius))
            
            # Get circles at current position
            curr_circles = []
            for x, y, z_center, r in spheres:
                radius = get_circle_radius((x, y, z_center, r), z_curr)
                if radius > 0:
                    curr_circles.append((x, y, radius))
            
            # Count connected components
            prev_components = get_connected_components(prev_circles)
            curr_components = get_connected_components(curr_circles)
            
            # Check if there's a transition
            if curr_components > prev_components:
                transitions.append('1')
            else:
                transitions.append('0')
        
        # Output
        m = len(transitions)
        print(m)
        print(''.join(transitions))
```

    if dz >= r:

    return math.sqrt(r*r - dz*dz)

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

In the year 2xxx, an expedition team landing on a planet found strange objects made by an ancient species living on that planet. They are transparent boxes containing opaque solid spheres (Figure 1). There are also many lithographs which seem to contain positions and radiuses of spheres.

<image>

Figure 1: A strange object

Initially their objective was unknown, but Professor Zambendorf found the cross section formed by a horizontal plane plays an important role. For example, the cross section of an object changes as in Figure 2 by sliding the plane from bottom to top.

<image>

Figure 2: Cross sections at different positions

He eventually found that some information is expressed by the transition of the number of connected figures in the cross section, where each connected figure is a union of discs intersecting or touching each other, and each disc is a cross section of the corresponding solid sphere. For instance, in Figure 2, whose geometry is described in the first sample dataset later, the number of connected figures changes as 0, 1, 2, 1, 2, 3, 2, 1, and 0, at z = 0.0000, 162.0000, 167.0000, 173.0004, 185.0000, 191.9996, 198.0000, 203.0000, and 205.0000, respectively. By assigning 1 for increment and 0 for decrement, the transitions of this sequence can be expressed by an 8-bit binary number 11011000.

For helping further analysis, write a program to determine the transitions when sliding the horizontal plane from bottom (z = 0) to top (z = 36000).



Input

The input consists of a series of datasets. Each dataset begins with a line containing a positive integer, which indicates the number of spheres N in the dataset. It is followed by N lines describing the centers and radiuses of the spheres. Each of the N lines has four positive integers Xi, Yi, Zi, and Ri (i = 1, . . . , N) describing the center and the radius of the i-th sphere, respectively.

You may assume 1 ≤ N ≤ 100, 1 ≤ Ri ≤ 2000, 0 < Xi - Ri < Xi + Ri < 4000, 0 < Yi - Ri < Yi + Ri < 16000, and 0 < Zi - Ri < Zi + Ri < 36000. Each solid sphere is defined as the set of all points (x, y, z) satisfying (x - Xi)2 + (y - Yi)2 + (z - Zi)2 ≤ Ri2.

A sphere may contain other spheres. No two spheres are mutually tangent. Every Zi ± Ri and minimum/maximum z coordinates of a circle formed by the intersection of any two spheres differ from each other by at least 0.01.

The end of the input is indicated by a line with one zero.

Output

For each dataset, your program should output two lines. The first line should contain an integer M indicating the number of transitions. The second line should contain an M-bit binary number that expresses the transitions of the number of connected figures as specified above.

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have transparent boxes containing opaque solid spheres
2. We're looking at cross-sections of these spheres at different z-levels (heights)
3. Each sphere creates a circle in the cross-section at height z: the set of points (x,y) such that (x-Xi)² + (y-Yi)² ≤ Ri² - (z-Zi)²
4. When circles overlap or touch, they form connected components
5. We need to track how the number of connected components changes as we move the horizontal plane from z=0 to z=36000
6. For each transition (when the number of connected components increases or decreases), we record 1 for increase and 0 for decrease
7. Output the total number of transitions and the binary string representing them

## Key Insights

1. The cross-section at height z contains circles centered at (Xi, Yi) with radius √(Ri² - (z-Zi)²)
2. When z is outside the valid range for a sphere (z < Zi - Ri or z > Zi + Ri), the circle doesn't exist
3. The interesting events happen at specific z-values where:
   - A new circle appears (z = Zi - Ri)
   - A circle disappears (z = Zi + Ri)
   - Two circles touch or intersect (when distance between centers equals sum of radii)
4. We need to sort all critical z-values and evaluate the number of connected components at each interval

## Algorithm Steps

1. Find all critical z-values (where circles appear/disappear or intersect)
2. For each interval between consecutive critical z-values, compute the number of connected components
3. Track the transitions (increases/decreases) between consecutive intervals
4. Output the count and binary representation of transitions

## Implementation

```python
import math
from collections import defaultdict

def distance(p1, p2):
    return math.sqrt((p1[0] - p2[0])**2 + (p1[1] - p2[1])**2)

def solve():
    while True:
        n = int(input())
        if n == 0:
            break
            
        spheres = []
        for _ in range(n):
            x, y, z, r = map(int, input().split())
            spheres.append((x, y, z, r))
        
        # Find all critical z-values
        critical_z = set()
        
        # Add z-values where circles appear/disappear
        for x, y, z, r in spheres:
            critical_z.add(z - r)
            critical_z.add(z + r)
        
        # Add z-values where pairs of circles intersect
        for i in range(len(spheres)):
            for j in range(i + 1, len(spheres)):
                x1, y1, z1, r1 = spheres[i]
                x2, y2, z2, r2 = spheres[j]
                
                # Distance between centers in xy plane
                d_xy = distance((x1, y1), (x2, y2))
                
                # Check if circles intersect (distance between centers equals sum or difference of radii)
                r_sum = r1 + r2
                r_diff = abs(r1 - r2)
                
                # If d_xy <= r1 + r2 and d_xy >= |r1 - r2|, they intersect
                if d_xy <= r_sum and d_xy >= r_diff:
                    # Find z-values where they touch
                    # At height z, the circles have radii sqrt(r1^2 - (z-z1)^2) and sqrt(r2^2 - (z-z2)^2)
                    # They touch when the distance between centers equals the sum of the two radii
                    # This gives a quadratic equation in z
                    
                    # The equation: sqrt(r1^2 - (z-z1)^2) + sqrt(r2^2 - (z-z2)^2) = d_xy
                    # This is complex to solve analytically, so we'll use numerical method
                    
                    # Instead, we'll find the z-values where the circles touch by solving the equation
                    # We know the critical z-values are where one circle appears/disappears or when two circles touch
                    
                    # For simplicity, we'll just add the z-values where circles first touch or stop touching
                    # We'll use the fact that for two intersecting circles, there are at most two z-values where they touch
                    
                    # Use binary search to find the z-values where circles touch
                    # The z-range is between the minimum and maximum z of the spheres
                    
                    z_min = max(z1 - r1, z2 - r2)
                    z_max = min(z1 + r1, z2 + r2)
                    
                    if z_min < z_max:
                        # We'll find bounds for where circles touch
                        # Actually, we need to find z where sqrt(r1^2 - (z-z1)^2) + sqrt(r2^2 - (z-z2)^2) = d_xy
                        
                        # The equation is complex, so we'll just use the fact that at x and y, the circles touch
                        # We'll add the endpoints of the intersection interval
                        
                        # The critical z-values occur at the boundaries of intersection
                        # We need to solve for z in: sqrt(r1^2 - (z-z1)^2) + sqrt(r2^2 - (z-z2)^2) = d_xy
                        
                        # This is difficult, so let's use a simpler approach - only track when circles appear/disappear
                        # and when they start/end intersecting (which happens at the edges of their valid z-intervals)
                        pass
        
        # Actually, let's simplify - we only need to consider events where circles appear, disappear, or when they touch
        # Since the problem states that every Zi ± Ri and intersection points differ by at least 0.01, 
        # we can just consider z-values where either a circle appears/disappears or when they touch
        
        # Let's reconsider: we need to find when two circles touch (distance between centers = sum of radii in 2D)
        
        critical_z = set()
        
        # Add z-values where circles appear/disappear
        for x, y, z, r in spheres:
            critical_z.add(z - r)
            critical_z.add(z + r)
        
        # For intersection events, we need to find where the circles touch
        # For two circles with centers (x1,y1,z1,r1) and (x2,y2,z2,r2)
        # At height z, radii are r1' = sqrt(r1^2 - (z-z1)^2) and r2' = sqrt(r2^2 - (z-z2)^2)
        # They touch when distance between centers (in xy plane) = r1' + r2'
        
        for i in range(len(spheres)):
            for j in range(i + 1, len(spheres)):
                x1, y1, z1, r1 = spheres[i]
                x2, y2, z2, r2 = spheres[j]
                
                d_xy = distance((x1, y1), (x2, y2))
                d_xy_sq = d_xy * d_xy
                
                # For circles to touch at height z: sqrt(r1^2 - (z-z1)^2) + sqrt(r2^2 - (z-z2)^2) = d_xy
                
                # If the circles don't intersect in 2D (d_xy > r1 + r2 or d_xy < |r1 - r2|), no touch
                if d_xy > r1 + r2 or d_xy < abs(r1 - r2):
                    continue
                    
                # We need to find z such that sqrt(r1^2 - (z-z1)^2) + sqrt(r2^2 - (z-z2)^2) = d_xy
                # This is a complex equation, but we can use binary search
                
                # The z-value must be in [max(z1-r1, z2-r2), min(z1+r1, z2+r2)]
                z_min = max(z1 - r1, z2 - r2)
                z_max = min(z1 + r1, z2 + r2)
                
                if z_min >= z_max:
                    continue
                    
                # Find the z-values where circles touch
                # We can use bisection to find the intersection points
                # Since the problem says they differ by at least 0.01, we can find the bounds
                
                low, high = z_min, z_max
                # We expect at most 2 intersection points (they can touch at one or two points)
                
                # Try to find one intersection point
                # The function f(z) = sqrt(r1^2 - (z-z1)^2) + sqrt(r2^2 - (z-z2)^2) - d_xy
                # We want to find where f(z) = 0
                
                # Use binary search
                for _ in range(100):  # 100 iterations for high precision
                    mid = (low + high) / 2.0
                    z1_z = r1 * r1 - (mid - z1) * (mid - z1)
                    z2_z = r2 * r2 - (mid - z2) * (mid - z2)
                    
                    if z1_z < 0 or z2_z < 0:
                        # z is outside valid range
                        if z1_z < 0:
                            low = mid
                        if z2_z < 0:
                            high = mid
                        continue
                    
                    r1_z = math.sqrt(z1_z)
                    r2_z = math.sqrt(z2_z)
                    
                    if r1_z + r2_z > d_xy:
                        high = mid
                    else:
                        low = mid
                
                # Add midpoint as a critical point (it might be approximate)
                if low < high:
                    critical_z.add(low)
                    critical_z.add(high)
        
        # Convert to sorted list
        critical_z = sorted(critical_z)
        
        # Add boundaries
        critical_z = [0.0] + critical_z + [36000.0]
        
        # Remove duplicates
        critical_z = list(set(critical_z))
        critical_z.sort()
        
        # For each interval, compute the number of connected components
        # First, we need a function to find connected components of circles at height z
        
        def get_connected_components(z):
            # For each sphere, compute the radius at height z
            circles = []
            for x, y, z_s, r in spheres:
                z_diff = z - z_s
                if z_diff >= r or z_diff <= -r:
                    continue  # Circle doesn't exist at this height
                radius = math.sqrt(r * r - z_diff * z_diff)
                circles.append((x, y, radius))
            
            # Use union-find or greedy approach to find connected components
            # For small number of circles, we can use a simple approach
            if not circles:
                return 0
                
            # Sort circles by x-coordinate
            circles.sort()
            
            # Use union-find to group touching circles
            components = []
            
            # Group by connected components
            # Two circles are connected if distance between centers <= sum of radii
            # Use a simple greedy approach: start from leftmost circle, and keep expanding
            
            used = [False] * len(circles)
            connected_components = 0
            
            for i in range(len(circles)):
                if used[i]:
                    continue
                    
                # Start a new component
                connected_components += 1
                # Find all circles that are connected to this one
                for j in range(i + 1, len(circles)):
                    if used[j]:
                        continue
                    
                    # Check if circles i and j touch or intersect
                    x1, y1, r1 = circles[i]
                    x2, y2, r2 = circles[j]
                    d = math.sqrt((x1 - x2) ** 2 + (y1 - y2) ** 2)
                    if d <= r1 + r2:
                        used[j] = True
            
            return connected_components
        
        # Now track transitions
        transitions = []
        
        # Go through each interval
        for i in range(len(critical_z) - 1):
            z1, z2 = critical_z[i], critical_z[i + 1]
            
            # Evaluate number of components at z1 (just after or just before)
            # Since we're dealing with continuous functions, we can evaluate at z1
            comp1 = get_connected_components(z1)
            
            # Evaluate at z2
            comp2 = get_connected_components(z2)
            
            # Check what happened between z1 and z2
            if comp2 > comp1:
                transitions.append(1)
            elif comp2 < comp1:
                transitions.append(0)
            # If comp2 == comp1, no transition (we don't count it)
        
        # Output
        print(len(transitions))
        if transitions:
            print(''.join(map(str, transitions)))
        else:
            print('0')
```

```python
def solve():

                        else:
                            high = mid

        def get_connected_components(z):

                z_diff = z - z_s
                if z_diff >= r or z_diff <= -r:
                    continue
                radius = math.sqrt(r * r - z_diff * z_diff)
                circles.append((x, y, radius))
            
            if not circles:
                return 0
                
            # Use union-find to find connected components
            n_circles = len(circles)
            parent = list(range(n_circles))
            
            def find(x):
                if parent[x] != x:
                    parent[x] = find(parent[x])
                return parent[x]
            
            def union(x, y):
                px, py = find(x), find(y)
                if px != py:
                    parent[px] = py
            
            # Check all pairs of circles
            for i in range(n_circles):
                for j in range(i + 1, n_circles):
                    x1, y1, r1 = circles[i]
                    x2, y2, r2 = circles[j]
                    d = distance((x1, y1), (x2, y2))
                    if d <= r1 + r2:
                        union(i, j)
            
            # Count unique components
            components = set()
            for i in range(n_circles):
                components.add(find(i))
            
            return len(components)
        
        # Track transitions
        transitions = []
        
        for i in range(len(critical_z) - 1):
            z1, z2 = critical_z[i], critical_z[i + 1]
            
            # Evaluate at z1 and z2
            comp1 = get_connected_components(z1)
            comp2 = get_connected_components(z2)
            
            # Note: we might have floating point precision issues
            # Since the problem guarantees differences are at least 0.01, 
            # we should get clean transitions
            
            if comp2 > comp1:
                transitions.append(1)
            elif comp2 < comp1:
                transitions.append(0)
        
        # Output
        print(len(transitions))
        if transitions:
            print(''.join(map(str, transitions)))
        else:
            print('0')
```

    return math.sqrt((p1[0] - p2[0])**2 + (p1[1] - p2[1])**2)

def solve():

        n = int(input())
        if n == 0:
            break
            
        spheres = []
        for _ in range(n):
            x, y, z, r = map(int, input().split())
            spheres.append((x, y, z, r))

        critical_z = set()
        
        # Add z-values where circles appear/disappear
        for x, y, z, r in spheres:
            critical_z.add(z - r)
            critical_z.add(z + r)
        
        # For intersection events, find where circles touch
        for i in range(len(spheres)):
            for j in range(i + 1, len(spheres)):
                x1, y1, z1, r1 = spheres[i]
                x2, y2, z2, r2 = spheres[j]
                
                # Distance between centers in xy plane
                d_xy = distance((x1, y1), (x2, y2))
                
                # Check if circles can touch (they do if |r1 - r2| <= d_xy <= r1 + r2)
                if d_xy >= r1 + r2 or d_xy <= abs(r1 - r2):
                    continue
                    
                # We need to find z where the sum of vertical radii equals the horizontal distance
                # sqrt(r1^2 - (z-z1)^2) + sqrt(r2^2 - (z-z2)^2) = d_xy
                
                # Find range of valid z
                z_min = max(z1 - r1, z2 - r2)
                z_max = min(z1 + r1, z2 + r2)
                
                if z_min >= z_max:
                    continue
                    
                # Use bisection to find the z value where circles touch
                # Try to find one intersection point
                low, high = z_min, z_max
                
                # We expect at most 2 intersection points, so we'll try to find both
                # First find one intersection point

                for _ in range(100):  # 100 iterations for precision
                    mid = (low + high) / 2.0
                    z_diff1 = mid - z1
                    z_diff2 = mid - z2

                        # z is outside valid range

                            low = mid
                        else:
                            high = mid
                        continue
                    
                    r1_z = math.sqrt(r1 * r1 - z_diff1 * z_diff1)
                    r2_z = math.sqrt(r2 * r2 - z_diff2 * z_diff2)

                    critical_z.add(high)

        critical_z = sorted(set(critical_z))
        
        # For each interval, compute the number of connected components
        def get_connected_components(z):
            # Find all circles at height z
            circles = []
            for x, y, z_s, r in spheres:
                z_diff = z - z_s
                if z_diff >= r or z_diff <= -r:
                    continue
                radius = math.sqrt(r * r - z_diff * z_diff)
                circles.append((x, y, radius))
            
            if not circles:
                return 0
                
            # Use union-find to find connected components
            n_circles = len(circles)
            parent = list(range(n_circles))
            
            def find(x):
                if parent[x] != x:
                    parent[x] = find(parent[x])
                return parent[x]
            
            def union(x, y):
                px, py = find(x), find(y)
                if px != py:
                    parent[px] = py
            
            # Check all pairs of circles
            for i in range(n_circles):
                for j in range(i + 1, n_circles):
                    x1, y1, r1 = circles[i]
                    x2, y2, r2 = circles[j]
                    d = distance((x1, y1), (x2, y2))
                    if d <= r1 + r2:
                        union(i, j)
            
            # Count unique components
            components = set()
            for i in range(n_circles):
                components.add(find(i))
            
            return len(components)
        
        # Track transitions
        transitions = []

                transitions.append(0)

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

In the year 2xxx, an expedition team landing on a planet found strange objects made by an ancient species living on that planet. They are transparent boxes containing opaque solid spheres (Figure 1). There are also many lithographs which seem to contain positions and radiuses of spheres.

<image>

Figure 1: A strange object

Initially their objective was unknown, but Professor Zambendorf found the cross section formed by a horizontal plane plays an important role. For example, the cross section of an object changes as in Figure 2 by sliding the plane from bottom to top.

<image>

Figure 2: Cross sections at different positions

He eventually found that some information is expressed by the transition of the number of connected figures in the cross section, where each connected figure is a union of discs intersecting or touching each other, and each disc is a cross section of the corresponding solid sphere. For instance, in Figure 2, whose geometry is described in the first sample dataset later, the number of connected figures changes as 0, 1, 2, 1, 2, 3, 2, 1, and 0, at z = 0.0000, 162.0000, 167.0000, 173.0004, 185.0000, 191.9996, 198.0000, 203.0000, and 205.0000, respectively. By assigning 1 for increment and 0 for decrement, the transitions of this sequence can be expressed by an 8-bit binary number 11011000.

For helping further analysis, write a program to determine the transitions when sliding the horizontal plane from bottom (z = 0) to top (z = 36000).



Input

The input consists of a series of datasets. Each dataset begins with a line containing a positive integer, which indicates the number of spheres N in the dataset. It is followed by N lines describing the centers and radiuses of the spheres. Each of the N lines has four positive integers Xi, Yi, Zi, and Ri (i = 1, . . . , N) describing the center and the radius of the i-th sphere, respectively.

You may assume 1 ≤ N ≤ 100, 1 ≤ Ri ≤ 2000, 0 < Xi - Ri < Xi + Ri < 4000, 0 < Yi - Ri < Yi + Ri < 16000, and 0 < Zi - Ri < Zi + Ri < 36000. Each solid sphere is defined as the set of all points (x, y, z) satisfying (x - Xi)2 + (y - Yi)2 + (z - Zi)2 ≤ Ri2.

A sphere may contain other spheres. No two spheres are mutually tangent. Every Zi ± Ri and minimum/maximum z coordinates of a circle formed by the intersection of any two spheres differ from each other by at least 0.01.

The end of the input is indicated by a line with one zero.

Output

For each dataset, your program should output two lines. The first line should contain an integer M indicating the number of transitions. The second line should contain an M-bit binary number that expresses the transitions of the number of connected figures as specified above.
